GAZBOT V7 — the Friday report

Week ending 2026-07-31  ·  MNQ-only  ·  PAPER  ·  REVISION 3, rebuilt 2026-08-01  ·  34 plays

What changed in Revision 3. Rev2 was folded in on 08-01 and only 2 of its 5 sections actually landed — the report went out still printing the numbers those sections were written to overturn. Rev3 lands all of them, applies the in-place corrections so a reader going top-to-bottom is never shown a superseded claim as current, and adds the three weekend re-derivations that overturn three of the corrections themselves. Nothing has been deleted: where a claim was overturned, the original is struck through and the correction sits next to it in a red box.

★ THE HONEST HEADLINE — the +$625 is not what the machine did

The desk booked +$625 over 206 trades this week and that is real money on a real ledger. But twelve of those trades you closed by hand (exit_reason = MANUAL_CLAIM), and those twelve booked +$1,223. Take your hands off and the machine — every gate, every exit, running unattended — did 194 fills for −$598 at 35.1% win, −$3.08 a trade, profit factor 0.91.

Cut of the same weekNet $N Win%What it is
Headline, as booked+625.0206 38.8%what the ledger says
Automated only — hands off −598.019435.1% PF 0.91, −$3.08/trade — the machine on its own
Your twelve hand-claims+1,223.0 12100%12 for 12
Those same twelve, repriced on 250 ms tape under the exit config actually loaded +360.512— five of the twelve would have stopped out
A fully hands-off week −237.5206— automated book + what the machine would have done with your twelve
So your hands were worth +862.5—— and it is invisible in every P&L we report — see BUILD #2

Twelve claims at 12-for-12 is not luck. It is a signal the machine is missing, and the honest reading of the week is that the entries and the automated exits did not pay; your discretion did. Only one gate — abs_veto — is positive on its own machinery. The gate this report originally crowned “the survivor”, exhaustion_short, is −$56 over 44 automated fires; $372.5 of its +$316 week was four of your clicks.

★ THE SCORECARD — four headline recommendations went into the weekend, one came out

Every one of these was independently re-derived from source over the weekend by an agent that had not written the original, and was actively trying to break it. Read this before you read anything else in the report — it is the most important thing the document has to tell you.

Friday's recommendationWhat it claimed Re-derived verdictWhere
Stop benching momentum in a confirmed TREND_UP — billed as “the highest-value, best-evidenced change in the whole report” +$1,615/week, positive in all nine robustness folds WITHDRAWN The arithmetic reproduces (+$1,644) and then falls apart three ways. The estimator books a gate into a trade it is already in — fix that and it is +$767; write the rule so it cannot buy dips in a downtrend and it is +$158 on n=4. A placebo test over 20,000 draws makes it null under a textbook ADX tag (p=0.56) and worse than random under a 60-minute regression tag (z=−2.56). And 52% of the money is two clusters of momentum longs bought into bounces on the week's biggest DOWN day. A confirmed trend was live for 3.3% of the week — there was nothing to ride. Part 3.5
grind_long exit → {a_r 0.5, b 1.0} Go tight: MED-TREND +$10.3/trade at 59% win WITHDRAWN Swept properly — 130 configs, 3,064 tick-repriced entries, 16 sessions, on a sequential one-position-per-slot sim — the cell is −$1,127 worse than what is loaded today and fails every robustness test. The direction is the opposite of what was recommended: tighter is worse. And a methodology bug fell out of it: the Friday exit lab's paired method stacks overlapping entries and scored the same config +$2,961 where sequential scores it −$2,115 — a $5,076 swing from method alone, which puts two other exit cells on this card under suspicion. Part 3.7
grind_long entry fix — drop the ER floor, ATR 24→12, ext_atr ≤ 2.0 £700/week; −$284 → +$2,830 WRONG BASELINE — the change still ships The −$284 baseline is a simulation of a config that was not running: the rehab replay applied floors the live desk has had switched off since 07-29. The honest before/after is +$1,552 → +$2,830, so about 59% of the advertised delta was removing filters that were already de-facto removed. And use ATR 10, not 12 — 9/10/11 is a real plateau, at 12 the blocked book flips positive, and 12 costs $941 against 10. The £/week is now £0: grind breaks even at a 1.64× loss multiplier and live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled. Part 3.6
Every ER and ATR floor on the desk has been dead since 07-29 A lookup bug: the floors are keyed on base names, the live slots carry _A/ _B suffixes CONFIRMED — three times, by three independent routes From source, from the journal, and from the live ledger (of 40 grind fills booked after the ER floor shipped, 31 had ER below it). Three agents reached the same bug by three different routes. One correction: the circulating “7,734 suppressions” is a raw log-line count — de-duplicated it is 383 blocked signal episodes over 8 days, 44× smaller. And the important half nobody had done: four of the five floors should never be switched back on, which is why the ship order matters more than the fix. Part 3.6
What that leaves standing

The fader bench. Of everything the router does, benching the counter-trend faders is the one claim that survives every test anyone could throw at it: +$810, n=25, non-overlap, and identical under every independent regime tag. Benching into CHOP does not make that grade — it is +$612 under the router's own tag, +$187 under ADX and −$382 under a regression tag, i.e. it only holds if you accept the router's own definition of chop. And the blended “router value” number quoted twice in this report (−$1,216) is double-counted twice over; merged it is −$440 to −$646, it flips sign on which single day you drop, and it should not be quoted at all.

The per-tick stop guard. 26 unfilled stops before, 0 unfilled and 103 clean stops after. Already in production. Do not revert it.

abs_veto. Cold re-validation on 238 trades: +$2,668 at 47% win, both sides green independently, 9 of 12 days green. It is the best thing on the board. It was never a promotion — it shipped two-sided on 07-25 — and what leaked was its arming policy, not the gate.

Deadline is the Sunday 22:00 UTC reopen.

2 · If you ship the floors tonight, ship them in order (SATURDAY #1). The values go first, while they are provably a no-op; the wiring fix goes last, as the single arming moment. Backwards costs about −$370/day and presents as “the desk mysteriously stopped trading”.

#What to doThe number behind itOwnerRevert path
1★ Dead ER/ATR floors — CONFIRMED, with the corrected per-gate values and the ship ORDERCONFIRMED from source. Mechanism in one sentence: tournament.step() passes the raw slot tag to er_blocks()/atr_blocks(), but under the dual-slot scale-out slate every tag carries an _A/_B suffix while ER_FLOOR/ATR_FLOOR are keyed on BASE names — the lookup never matches, so every floor silently returns 'don't block'. The _base() helper at tournament.py:86 is applied at lines 250/303/345 but NOT at 139/142. Dead since 2026-07-29 16:20:49 UTC (commit 5b0913b + the scaleout.conf drop-in); last suppression line 07-29T13:29:04Z, zero since.

SHIP IN THIS ORDER — the order is the whole trick, because while the floors are dead, changing their VALUES is provably a no-op:
1. deciders.py values first: ER_FLOOR = {} (delete all), ATR_FLOOR = {grind_long: 10.0, capitulation_long: 10.0}. Zero live effect today. Revert: restore two literals.
2. THEN tournament.py:139/142 -> _base(slot). THIS is the arming moment. Verify grind blocks ~10% of fires, capitulation ~43%, and ZERO 'ER gate:' lines. Revert: one line — this is the kill switch for 1+2 together.
3. THEN slot_strategy.py:118 add "ext_hi": 2.0 — separate commit and restart. The only step with immediate unconditional effect (~39% fewer grind fires). Revert: delete the key.
4. Nothing else. The abs_veto_short ER>=0.10 and capitulation ER>=0.25 leads go to SHADOW.
Nuclear revert throughout: GAZBOT7_TOURNAMENT_SLATE=tournament.

Standing caveat on every dollar on this card

Every repriced number in this report is a CEILING. Fills are modelled at the exact stop/target/trail level with no slippage, and the desk's own standing finding is that live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled. Where a change breaks even at a loss multiplier inside that band — grind_long at 1.64× is the live example — treat the policy as the edge and the headline dollar as decoration. Anything on thin n is flagged in-row as a direction, not a measurement.

§ The evidence behind those plays

Everything from here down is the working. Two halves, one document. The warm half is what the live six-gate paper tournament actually did this week (Part 1), then the section that matters most — Rehabilitation (Part 1.5), where every red gate and leaky exit is walked through the full six-step wash instead of being benched at face value, graves and all. Then the shadow board and the promotion it earned plus the regime-flex exit lab (Part 2), the mid-week leads re-run on the week's own data (Part 2.5), and the Router Operation Review (Part 2.6). Part 3 is the new material: the Rev2 answers that never landed and the three Rev3 re-derivations that overturn three of them. Then the cold, tape-first half: we ignore everything the desk did and ask, from the raw MNQ tape and order book, where was the money and did we show up? (M1) — then test whether the gates we already own could have caught the runs we missed (M2), and finally build brand-new gates from scratch and backtest them to death, the ones that failed included, that is the point (M3). Plain English, honest money, every claim a table — and every reprice a ceiling.

Part 1 The Live Desk — the week the router earned its keep

▶ Read this section with REV3 open. Three claims in here were overturned this weekend: exhaustion_short is not the week's earner once your hands come off it, grind's "0.35 ER floor" has never executed, and Thursday's +$1,172 record is a session-boundary artefact of nine hand-claims. The corrections are marked inline in red where they bite, and the full working is in Part 3 — the Rev2/Rev3 corrections. Nothing has been deleted: where a claim was overturned, the original is still printed next to what replaced it.
★ READ THIS FIRST — the automated desk lost money this week

The +$625 headline below is real, but it is not what the machine did on its own. Twelve of the week's 206 trades were closed by your own hand (exit_reason = MANUAL_CLAIM), and those twelve booked +$1,223. Strip them out and the automated desk — every gate, every exit, running unattended — is −$598 over 194 trades at 35.1% win. This was flagged as a caveat in an earlier draft but never computed; here it is.

So exhaustion_short's "green on 4 of 5 days" is hand-claim dependent — unattended it is roughly flat-to-negative (−$56 over 44 fires). Only abs_veto is positive on its own machinery. Everything downstream in this report that leans on exhaustion being "the steadiest earner" should be read against that. This is the single most important number in the week and it argues the opposite of the headline: the entries and the automated exits did not pay; your discretionary claims did.

Garrath — the 6-gate paper tournament made +$625 across 206 trades this week (Mon 27th through Fri 31st, on the Paris session-day boundary the desk keeps its books on). That headline hides a wild ride: one best-ever day (+$1,172 on Thursday) ▶ REV3: +$1,172 on the Paris boundary; +$94 on the UTC day (Movement 3 books it that way), and −$884.5 of it is automated — nine hand-claims are what made it a record., one clean two-way trend day (+$638 Wednesday), a near-scratch Tuesday, and two red days that bookended the week — a choppy Monday (−$227) and an ugly, whippy Friday (−$905). The money came from exactly two places: the gates that ride a real move (grind and abs_veto on the trend legs) and the gate that fades a tired one (exhaustion_short). Everything else either scratched or bled, and the router spent the week benching the bleeders. Below is the whole thing, every number computed off the tournament trades, honest about the fact that it is one week of in-sample paper.

Read this first — what SURVIVED the week. If you strip away the noise, one gate stood up on almost every kind of day: exhaustion_short was green on 4 of the 5 sessions (+$14, +$90, +$95, +$217, and only −$100 on Friday's violence), 54% of its trades won, +$316 for the week. ▶ REV3: $372.5 of that +$316 was four trades you closed by hand. Automated it is −$56 over 44 fires (see the callout above). It is the steadiest thing on the desk, not the biggest earner: 50% win, a median trade of essentially zero, and the smallest leave-one-day-out damage of any gate that traded in size. The flashier numbers — grind's +$704 Thursday, abs_veto_short's +$570 Wednesday — are real but they are day-type bets: they printed on the one or two days the tape actually trended and gave money back the rest of the time. Lead with the steady one; treat the big ones as conditional.

The week, day by day

Every day scored on the desk's own Paris session boundary, so these match the numbers in your ledger (Thursday's +$1,172 is the best single day the desk has ever booked on this boundary — Movement 3 books the same trades on the UTC calendar day and gets +$94; same week total, different day buckets). The "character" column is the router's live regime read, not a number I invented — it's what actually drove the bench/arm calls.

The shape of that week is the whole story: the desk does not need to win most days, it needs to be on the right gates on the two days that trend and out of the way on the days that don't. Thursday and Wednesday together made +$1,811; the other three days gave −$1,186 of it back. Net +$625.

This is the tournament roster this week: two "ride it" momentum gates (grind_long, abs_veto's long and short sides), two faders (exhaustion_short, capitulation_long) and the router-managed rgv_short. Each gate runs two sub-slots on one signal — a Lot-A scalp and a Lot-B chandelier tail — but here they're totalled to the whole gate. Two things jump out. First, grind is a low-win, big-winner machine — it only won 32% of its trades but its average winner (+$125) is more than double its average loser (−$54); it loses small a lot and wins big occasionally, which is exactly what a trend-rider should look like. Second, rgv_short barely fired at all (3 trades) — the router had it benched for almost the whole week because it kept trying to short an up-biased tape, and the 3 that slipped through all stopped. That's not a broken gate, that's the router correctly keeping it in the dugout.

Where the money actually came from — the exit mix

This is the most honest picture of the week. The stops are enormous in aggregate — the desk paid over six thousand dollars in stop-outs — but that's the price of admission: you take a lot of small stops to be present for the few big tails. The green all comes from three exits: the chandelier tails, the fixed targets, and the trades you claimed by hand.

The lesson baked in here: the tail is the edge. When you split each gate into its scalp lot (Lot A) and its chandelier tail lot (Lot B), the tail carries nearly all the money — grind's tail made +$302 while its scalp made +$31; abs_veto_long's tail +$322 vs its scalp +$5; abs_veto_short's tail +$380 vs +$52. The scalp lots roughly break even and lock in certainty; the tail lots are where the week was won.

Case 1 Thursday — the best day ever, and the router in the ring

The morning ramp is where the money was made. Note the five-hour gap after ~16:00 — the router benched the book into the roll-over and the desk simply stopped trading.

Thursday (+$1,172) is the week's headline and its best case study, because it was not a clean trend — it was an up-biased tape that topped out around lunchtime and then whipped violently for hours, and the desk still booked its best day ever by staying on the right gates through a dozen regime flips. This is the day to study when you want to know what the router is actually for.

How Thursday was won

The morning trended up and grind rode it — grind_long made +$704 across 46 trades on the day (32 of them UTC-Thursday), catching the up-legs with its chandelier tail. Then around 13:50 UTC the buy-climax printed (aggressor delta peaked, then buy intensity collapsed to a third), the tape rolled over, and the router flipped the whole book: benched the momentum longs and armed the short-side reversion faders. The result was the cleanest offensive sequence of the week — exhaustion_short banked four fade-claims in a row for +$372.5 (+$117, +$107.5, +$109.5, +$38.5) fading the roll-over. It then gave −$155 back on two later fires when the chop tried to resume up, netting +$217 on the day — but that four-in-a-row is the exhaustion edge on full display: judge this gate on its live fades, not its fixed-target shadow.

★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — those four fades were four hand-clicks, and the machine would have lost on them

All four were MANUAL_CLAIM exits — you closed them. Repriced on 250 ms tape under the exit config that was actually loaded at the time, the same four entries come out at −$51.2, and three of the four were sitting on positions the tape later ran 49 to 98 points through the stop. So the router's best-graded offensive call of the week (Part 2.6 §2 credits it +$372) is worth −$51 to the machine. That is a $423 swing inside the router's own scorecard.

Thursday's engineNet $NNote
grind_long (up-legs)+70446Rode the morning trend + the afternoon resumptions with the chandelier tail.
abs_veto_long+24235The veto-protected long — caught the confirmed breaks, sat out the fakes.
exhaustion_short (roll-over fade)+2176The 4-claim run +$372.5 fading the top, minus 2 late stops.
The two calls that went wrong on Thursday — and why they're worth more than the wins. The router made two genuinely bad calls this day, both caught and both banked into the desk's rulebook. (1) At 13:36 it benched grind on the US-open flush — the day gave back a chunk in 90 seconds and the router read grind as churning. You overrode it ("liven grind back up"), grind then rode to new highs and booked +$234/+$197 targets. Lesson banked: don't bench the aligned trend-rider on open stop-outs when the day is cleanly trending — the open is structurally violent, that's noise, only bench if the trend structure actually breaks. (2) At 15:49 it flipped to longs on a delta-blip in ER-0.01 dead chop — a four-bucket buyer build and a +45-point poke of the range top. It faded in two minutes. You caught it ("why is grind buying into chop"). Lesson banked: re-arm momentum only on a real break with ER actually climbing and vol expanding, never on a delta blip alone. Neither mistake cost real money (both got salvaged to roughly +$55), but the process errors are the ones that write the rules.

The one call that clearly saved money

At 14:51, when ER collapsed from 0.40 to 0.07 over two ticks and price rolled off the highs, the router proactively benched both momentum longs while the desk was flat — not reacting to stops, reading the regime flip ahead of them. Over the window that followed, the momentum shadows that would have kept firing lost about −$400 (thrust_aligned −$202, thrust_cont −$118, thrust_loose −$78). The desk sat flat and protected instead. That single defensive read is the clearest example of the router paying for itself: it's cheap to be wrongly benched (you miss a few trades), expensive to be wrongly armed (you churn), and this call landed on the right side of that asymmetry.

Case 2 Wednesday — the clean two-way trend, both sides caught

Both directions caught. The down-leg then the up-leg, with the desk on the right side of each.

Wednesday (+$638 on just 17 trades) is the "when it's real, take it and go home" day. The tape ran a strong down-leg and then a strong up-leg, and — unusually — the desk caught both directions cleanly.

abs_veto_short's day

The star was abs_veto_short: 10 trades, +$570.5, riding the down-leg. It only won 4 of those 10 — but one of them was a +$536 chandelier runner, the single best trade of the entire week, and that one tail dwarfed the small stops around it. This is the abs_veto shape in one trade: the 55-second continuation-veto keeps it out of the fake thrusts, and when it does catch a real one, the chandelier lets it run. Then late in the session the tape turned and trended up cleanly; the router benched the shorts (correctly — you don't short a strong up-move) and let the longs ride. Both legs, both green, 17 trades total. That's the ideal day and it's worth remembering it looks nothing like Thursday's 91-trade brawl.

Case 3 Friday — the enable-all-at-open bleed

The opposite picture: entries scattered through a whippy range with no trend to ride.

Friday (−$905) is the honest graveyard of the week and the sharpest single lesson in it. The US open printed a real up-thrust (ER 0.68, a clean break to 28713) and the book was set to "all six gates on" for the open. The thrust then fully reversed — a −172-point round-trip, ER collapsing from 0.50 to 0.12 into ATR-62 violence — and the wrong gates were holding the bag.

The open, gate by gate (13:30–14:30 UTC)

Gate at the openNet $StopsWhat happened
abs_veto_long (veto-protected)+146.5—Caught the real thrust, banked it, then its veto kept it OUT of the reversal.
exhaustion_short (off-regime)−72.01Fired the new 0.5R/1.5R scalp trial into ATR-62 — a scalp can't cover a 62-point stop.
grind_long (no veto)−284.04Bought the rollover top, walled — four stops in ninety seconds.

The contrast is the whole lesson, and it's now a banked rule: at a violent open, arm the veto-gates, not the churners. abs_veto's 55-second confirmation made it +$146 by only taking the thrust that continued; grind, with no such filter, chased the reversal and lost −$284. The rest of the day was a choppy recovery (ER 0.24–0.30 — elevated but not clean), and two attempts to re-arm abs_veto into it churned ≈−$245 more before you called it and went fully flat by 19:08. Going flat was the right end-state: the live shadows confirmed every benched gate would have kept bleeding through the ATR-52/62 chop. Some days the winning move is no move.

One Friday lead for the shadow desk (Part 2): the only configuration that made money on Friday's waterfall was rg_long_fast at +$249, fading the oversold flush and buying the capitulation dips — while our live long-fader, capitulation_long, bled. That's a flag that the right flush-catcher might be rg_long_fast, not capitulation. Chased down properly in the shadow section.

The STOP_UNFILLED self-heals

Six trades this week exited on STOP_UNFILLED — the known issue where IBKR won't fire a resting stop's trigger on the continuous contract. Every one of them was an abs_veto trade, and here's the important part: the stop-breach guard caught all six and bounded each loss to roughly one ATR. None ran naked, none turned into a drift-halt. That's the self-heal working as designed.

So the software failure cost about −$274 this week, all of it bounded and none of it catastrophic. The fix is already deployed and verified — see Part 1.5: commit d0218c6 went live 07-28 08:34 and there have been zero unfilled stops since, across 103 clean stop exits. The concrete-front-month branch is now moot. The guard remains the safety net underneath and it held six times out of six.

The router The direction-router trial — how it behaved

This was the week the Claude-run router stopped being a session experiment and became durable infrastructure — a headless systemd tick went live Friday that manages the desk even with no chat session running. Across the week the router changed the gate switches 59 times across 590 five-minute ticks (90% of ticks were no-change; 2 of the 59 came from the durable systemd tick, the rest from the session). It is benching-only, fully reversible, and paper — it can only block new entries, never force a trade — so its worst case is a missed opportunity, not a loss.

The router's own scorecard

Every switch change gets graded in the bad-call ledger once the outcome is known. The running tally sits at 17 GOOD · 2 BAD · 4 CHURN · 2 NEUTRAL. The honest read: most calls helped or cost nothing, the two outright bad calls both happened on Thursday and both were caught within minutes and turned into permanent rules, and the "churn" entries are process-thrash (net roughly zero in dollars) rather than money errors.

GradeCountMeaning & the notable ones
✅ GOOD17Calls that helped or avoided loss — incl. the Thu 14:51 regime-flip bench (≈−$400 churn avoided) and arming the chop-book that produced exhaustion's +$372.5 run.
❌ BAD2Both on Thu: benching the trend-rider on the open flush, and the premature delta-blip flip to longs. Both banked as rules.
⚠ CHURN4Over-toggling grind ~8× on Thursday morning; the Sunday-reopen "all six on"; two Friday re-enables into a choppy recovery. Thrashy, ≈$0 net.
◻ NEUTRAL2Veto-protected arms that never fired = zero cost, correct mechanism choice.

What the router got right, in plain terms

Strip out the drama and the router did its core job well: it kept the counter-trend faders benched almost the entire week (rgv_short fired only 3 times all week and abs_veto_short was correctly held out of the Friday chop after Wednesday's win), and it read the two big regime flips — Thursday's roll-over and Friday's reversal — closely enough to sit flat through the worst churn. Both green days (Wed +$638, Thu +$1,172) happened under the router. Its recurring weakness is the same one all week and in the ledger: over-toggling grind tick-to-tick and re-arming momentum a beat too early on elevated-but-not-clean chop. The fix is discipline, not code — arm grind once on a genuinely strong trend, leave it, and wait for ER to actually climb before believing a break.

Honest caveats — read before you trust any of this. This is one week of in-sample paper trading, and a lopsided one: two-thirds of the week's +$625 net came from a single best-ever day, and a big chunk of the green (+$1,223 across 12 trades) was booked by your own manual claims, not by the exits firing on their own — so the automated system alone did less than the headline. The per-gate reads are built on thin counts (rgv_short fired 3 times; exhaustion's new scalp trial only 11), so treat "abs_veto_short is a down-leg catcher" as a one-week observation, not a proven edge — it rests heavily on a single +$536 runner. The router's scorecard is self-graded. And capitulation_long (−$139) and the new exhaustion 0.5R/1.5R scalp trial (−$48.5, fired off-regime into Friday's violence) both go into Part 1.5's rehab bench rather than getting a face-value bench here — the honest "how could this work" comes next.

1.5 Live-Desk Rehabilitation — never bench a gate at face value

Garrath — this is the section you care about most, so here is the deal up front. Nothing on this desk gets binned because it had a red week. Every red gate and every leaky exit got walked through the full six steps — what looked broken, what would the loss have been if the plumbing worked, is it the base / the exit / the signal, what fixes were tried (and the winners-kept test each one had to pass), does it survive stripping the best days and dropping any single day, and finally a verdict. This week five targets went through the wash. Three came out fixed, one is a chop-only specialist, one goes back to the bench to earn its keep in shadow, and nothing earned a retirement. Then — because the honest failures are the whole point — every dead end from this week's attempts is laid out in the graveyard at the bottom. We earn your trust by showing you the graves, not by hiding them.

The one rule behind everything below
A fix that "wins" only by throwing away the big tail winners is a fake win and gets rejected on sight. Every survivor here had to keep the winners and cut the bleed. Every number is from a real tick-honest replay ($2/pt, $1.50 a round-turn per contract) on this week's tape plus, wherever possible, a second different regime — never a made-up figure, never a blanket cross-tape average (that averages the days a gate should be on with the days it should be off, and lies).
▶ Read this section with REV3 open. The grind_long rehab's headline £-figure was measured against a baseline that was never running. The honest before/after is +$1,552 → +$2,830, and the ATR floor should be 10, not 12. The corrections are marked inline in red where they bite, and the full working is in Part 3 — the Rev2/Rev3 corrections. Nothing has been deleted: where a claim was overturned, the original is still printed next to what replaced it.

★ What got rehabilitated — the survivors

1. STOP_UNFILLED — FIXED and already live (the strongest win of the week)

This was flagged as "the single worst exit-mechanism bleed of the week" — four stops that never filled, −$195. Two things turned out to be true, and both matter.

First, most of the headline is mislabelled. A stop that fires late is still mostly a normal stop-loss the losing entry earned anyway. Of the −$195, only −$53 is the actual defect (the extra few points lost while the trigger sat there); the other −$142 is ordinary stop-loss the entries would have taken with a perfect stop. Across the whole archive it's the same shape — only 24% of the bleed was ever the bug.

WindowSTOP_UNFILLEDclean STOP
before the fix (≤ 07-28 08:34)26—
at / after the fix0103

Second, it is already fixed and verified in production. The winning change (d0218c6, live 07-28 08:34) checks every tick (~2s) and market-flattens at the stop, so stops no longer depend on the broker's trigger firing at all. The last unfilled stop in the entire book was 32 minutes before that deploy. On real ticks the 2s path actually beats a "perfect" stop by ~$15 (fast bounces come back inside 2s). Two earlier attempts (concrete-Future stops, fillable StopLimit) helped but did not kill it — shown honestly in the graveyard. No action needed; do not revert.

2. grind_long — FIXED at the entry, and it holds in a second regime

Your exact symptom — "it buys resistance and session-highs and churns the chop" — is real and now explained. The bleed is 100% the entry signal, not the exit. Every dollar of profit lives in the exit tail; every dollar of loss lives in stopped entries:

Why the raw number liesn$
STOP (the entries get stopped — the bleed is here)53−2,718
TARGET (Lot-A scalps)9+1,137
CHANDELIER + MANUAL_CLAIM (Lot-B tail)16+1,567
★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — every ER and ATR floor on this desk has been switched off since 2026-07-29

Nothing below that leans on a live ER or ATR floor is describing the desk you own. tournament.step() hands the sub-slot tag (grind_long_A) to er_blocks()/atr_blocks(), but ER_FLOOR/ATR_FLOOR are keyed on base names, so the lookup never matches and every floor silently answers "don't block". Dead since the dual-slot cutover at 2026-07-29 16:20:49 UTC; last suppression line 07-29T13:29:04Z, zero since. Five gates affected. The _base() helper that fixes it already exists at tournament.py:86 and is applied correctly three times elsewhere — just not at lines 139 and 142.

Size it honestly: the circulating "7,734 suppressions" is a raw log-line count and the check re-fires every ~1s tick. De-duplicated it is 383 blocked signal episodes over 8 days (140 ER + 243 ATR) — about 48 a day, not thousands. Real, but 44× smaller than advertised.

And it changes the conclusion, not just the footnote. Grind did not fire 52 of 56 times below its floor because "ER filters are fake on this tape" — a claim about the market. It fired because there was no floor — a claim about our code. Those point at completely different fixes. Both ER floors were even committed ~18 hours after the bug, so neither has ever blocked a single trade in production. Full working, per-gate verdicts and the mandatory ship order: Part 3.6.

Two floors are configured against this gate — and neither has executed since 07-29, so they cannot have destroyed anything this week; as written they are backwards and too tight, which is exactly why they must not be re-wired at their current values. The ER ≥ 0.35 floor is backwards — a fast-slope gate catches a trend at birth, when the 30-min ER still reads the prior chop, so 26 of the 30 big tail-winners enter at low ER. The ATR ≥ 24 floor is far too tight for this regime. Swap both for a single "don't buy when already stretched" ceiling (ext_atr ≤ 2.0) and the gate flips right over:

★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — read the n before you read the delta, and use ATR 10

This is not the same eleven trades made profitable. The recommended config trades ten times as often — it is a different, ten-times-larger book (−$26/trade vs +$25/trade). And the "before" row is a counterfactual: the rehab replay applied the floors as config (rehab_grind_long.py:124, :234) while the live desk has had them switched off since 07-29. Measured against what was really running, the honest before/after is +$1,552 → +$2,830, not −$284 → +$2,830 — about 59% of the advertised delta is the removal of filters that were already de-facto removed.

Ship ATR 10, not 12. Re-derived fine on 8 days of tick-honest replay: 9/10/11 is a genuine plateau where the blocked book is robustly negative; at 12 the blocked book flips positive and stays positive all the way up. Twelve is one notch the wrong side of the cliff and costs $941 against 10. On every measure — net, per-signal, win%, strip-best-3, worst leave-one-day-out and both out-of-sample halves — ATR 10 + ext_atr 2.0 beats ATR 12 + ext_atr 2.0.

And the money is not bankable. Grind is a high-count, low-margin book: the best config breaks even at a 1.64× loss multiplier and the desk's standing finding is that live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled. That break-even sits inside the band. The ceiling buys resilience (at 1.5× losses: capped +$358 vs uncapped −$1,449), not dollars. Grind is being made less bad, not good — which is why the play card for this now carries £0, not £700.

Config (tick-honest, same tape)nnet$/tradewin%strip top-3
LIVE as written (ER≥.35 & ATR≥24) — a config that was never actually running11−$284−$2618%−$1,161
▶ REV3 — LIVE as actually running (floors dead since 07-29, ext_atr uncapped)181+$1,552+$8.634%—
RECOMMENDED (drop ER floor, ATR 24→12 10, ext_atr ≤ 2.0)111+$2,830+$2534%+$1,172

It survives stripping the best trades, survives dropping any single day (no day carries it), and — the clincher — it holds in a genuinely different regime: on the V5 summer-chop archive the same fix turns −$1,135 into +$172. That is the multi-regime proof the 07-30 ER-floor note explicitly asked for, and it points the opposite way to that floor. The exit (Lot-A 2.5R + Lot-B wide lock-chandelier) is proven and stays untouched. Honest caveat: this is a positive-skew tail gate — the top ~10 trades carry it — so treat the policy as the edge, not any single number.

3. chandelier runner give-back — FIXED per regime

This is the desk's biggest gross winner (+$4,774 on the CHANDELIER exit) but it leaks the tail — across all managed-profit exits the desk banks only 47% of peak, leaving ~$8,088 on the table. Your symptom trade reproduces exactly: 07-27 grind runner, peak 4.20R / $208, banked +$23 — gave back $185.

The cause is one knob used everywhere: the firm lock at lock_r=6.0. But the typical MNQ runner peaks at ~4.5R — below 6.0 — so on most runs the lock never engages and the position rides the wide trail all the way back down. The fix is to make the lock regime-aware:

Regime (entry 30m ER)incumbent wide, IS→OOS /sigproven fixaction
TREND (ER≥0.50)+52.2 → +46.9keep lock_r=6.0keep the wide lock — it's the edge
BUILD (0.30–0.50)+12.7 → −9.5 (overfit)lock_r=3.0 (+4.6 OOS)lower the lock
CHOP (ER<0.30)+4.1 → −1.2none robustrouter benches

On the actually-taken live trades this regime policy reconstructs +$3,580 vs the incumbent's +$2,932 (+22%) — the entire gain in chop/build, trend left completely alone. It drops zero entries; it just banks the tail earlier off-trend while keeping every trend monster on the wide trail. The machinery to deliver it already exists — it just isn't wired into the one regime-blind path (the dual-slot Lot-B "wide" tail on grind_long). Caveat: the trend leg is thin (n=4 live), so re-measure after the next clean-trend week; the build fix is the sturdier leg (robust 4/4 weeks + out-of-sample).

4. capitulation_long — FIXED, but it's a chop-only specialist

Live it shows −$139 this week. Tick-honest and single-lot, the current config is already roughly breakeven (+$24) — the extra loss is execution, not signal: the dual-slot A/B split doubled every chop stop (−$201 on just 4 signals), plus some low-vol-grind fires the router should have benched. But there is a real config fix underneath, and it reverses one of our own priors — the 07-25 rehab installed require_flip=True as "the edge". Tick-honest data says otherwise:

Confignnetwin%strip best-3worst day-drop
require_flip=True, 2.0R (current live)11+$2445%——
require_flip=False, 1.0R (recommended)32+$43478%+$327+$220

Waiting for the delta to flip enters late and skips the bounces. Fading the flush itself and banking a fast 1R (78% of bounces reach 1R; only ~40% reach 2R) is the least data-starved, most robust config in the whole study — every single-day-drop stays strongly green. Restrict it to overnight (fade small flushes when the tape is quiet, bench the US cash session where a fader gets run over) and it's +$422 at 81% win. The hard caveat: the tick tape is one chop week — there is no trend week in it to exercise the fader's predicted trend-day bleed. So ship the config, keep the router's down-trend and US benches (they are load-bearing, not optional), and confirm the benched side in shadow.

This is the MONDAY #3 play.

This one did not get fixed, and the honest verdict is SHADOW, not retirement. The honest small print
The tick tape is roughly one summer week (plus a second archive regime where we could reach for one). It is a chop-heavy tape with no violent US-open week and, for the faders, no clean trend week to exercise their predicted trend-day bleed. So: grind_long's fix is cross-regime proven and safe to ship; the chandelier and capitulation fixes are strongly proven in regime but lean on thin trend-side evidence and stay paired with router benches; rgv_short and both shadow watches are simply too thin to conclude and go to the bench / shadow to earn it. Nothing here is a cross-regime law — the ones that ship (grind entry ceiling, STOP_UNFILLED guard) are the ones that survived a second regime or are already running clean in production.

PART 2 The Shadow Desk & Promotion

This section was built from scratch on this week's numbers — no gate, candidate or verdict was inherited from last week's report. I ranked the whole shadow board on honest tick-repriced dollars (what a trade would really have made after slippage and fees, never the optimistic sim price), and let the promotion pick fall out of that ranking rather than deciding it in advance. Then I ran the full robustness battery on whatever came top, presented the two grind-exit shadow ledgers exactly as they stand tonight, and re-proved the exit ladder on the week's tick path per regime. Where the sample is thin I say so plainly — the shadow board nominates, the paper desk judges.

Relegation view — tune the SIDE, never bin a direction

Every "bleed" below is a side-and-regime problem, not a broken gate.

Grind exit shadow — 2R-partial smoothness

The 2R-partial is the only exit lever that survived earlier scrutiny: Lot A banks a 2R scalp for certainty, Lot B rides the pure-6.0 chandelier for the tail. Because grind is base-size 2 it's free to run. It was framed as a variance lever (smoother equity for a small mean give-up), not a mean-raiser — so it's the operator's call, not a pass/fail.

Metric (07-26 → 08-01)Baseline (deployed 2-lot chandelier)2R-partial (shadow)

Daily std $0 (1 day)0 (1 day)
Max drawdown $−278−214
Worst day $−278−214
Green-day % / green-week %0 / 00 / 0

Where the +$64 came from, trade by trade: on the three losers the partial is identical to baseline (both stop out); the whole difference is the one runner, where banking Lot A at 2R (+$130.5) beat letting both lots ride the chandelier back down (+$66).

Verdict: no new evidence to make the call on. operator's choice, unchanged The smoothness case is a claim about daily variance and drawdown across many days — and this window has one grind day and four trades, so daily std is zero and the whole variance thesis is untestable right now. What we can say: this week the partial edged the baseline by $64, entirely by scalping the single runner rather than riding it. That is the opposite of a mean give-up (this week it was a mean gain), purely because the lone runner topped out at 4.2R and the chandelier gave the tail back. One day proves nothing either way; the mean-vs-smoothness choice waits for a multi-day trend sample.

PART 2 · LAB The Regime-Flex Exit Lab

The desk's live control is now two files: gate_switches.env decides which gates are on, and exit_overrides.json decides the exit style per gate. This lab does two jobs — grade the live exhaustion_short fade-scalp trial on its actual fills, and re-prove the operator's regime-graded exit ladder by repricing every gate's real entries on the forward tick/5s path, segmented by regime rung, so the deployable exit_overrides policy is proven rather than guessed.

(a) The live exhaustion_short fade-scalp trial — on its actual fills

Deployed ~07:55 UTC on 07-31: Lot A banks at 0.5R, Lot B at 1.5R, replacing the prior A@2.5R + chandelier. Here is literally every leg it has filled since.

Time (UTC)legexit$
07-31 13:52A (0.5R)TARGET+51.5
07-31 13:52B (1.5R)STOP−123.5
07-31 18:41A (0.5R)STOP−27.5
Verdict: keep running — two signals is not a grade. too thin to judge The trial has fired exactly two signals (three legs) for a net of −$99.5. The one thing you can see: on the 13:52 signal the fast 0.5R Lot A banked (+$51.5) while the 1.5R Lot B rode past its cushion and stopped (−$123.5) — i.e. the fade reversed after the quick scalp, which is exactly the case the "bank A early" logic is meant for, except Lot B still gave it back. The 18:41 signal stopped on the A leg. For honest comparison the prior era (07-27–07-31, A@2.5R + chandelier, 37 trades) netted +$365 with a healthy 11 targets / 10 chandeliers / 16 stops mix. ▶ REV3: the prior era was not the same machine, and that mislabel hides the whole causal story. Until Wed 07-29 19:10 UTC exhaustion ran a single slot on the regime-3 adaptive chandelier: 37 automated fills, +$365, PF 1.46, green on all 3 of its days. The scale-out slate that replaced it (A@2.5R + wide lock-chandelier) is −$421 over 7 fills at 14% win. The gate did not break; its exit was swapped for a trend-riding one, and a fader does not get 2.5R in a whipsaw.
★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — the Rs can be graded even though the live trial cannot

A 64-entry 250 ms sweep does grade them: the loaded 0.5R Lot A is the worst scalp rung on the ladder (+$306); the plateau is 0.75–1.0R (+$510 / +$450); and Lot B's steadiest config by a distance is a k1.5 chandelier (+$502, the highest leave-one-day-out floor in the whole sweep at +$330, worst day −$25). That is the MONDAY #4 play.

But the sweep's real message is that no exit is robustly green in violent whipsaw, where this gate took 28 of its 62 entries — the k1.5 chandelier is the worst config in that bucket at −$202. Keeping it benched there is worth more than any R.

Method caveat added 08-01: this cell came from a per-signal sweep on overlapping entries — the same accounting that was just shown to swing grind's exit verdict by $5,076 (Part 3.7). Not refuted; re-run it under a sequential one-position-per-slot sim before trusting the size of the gain. Cheap to revert either way.

(b) Proving the exit ladder — best exit per gate × regime rung

I repriced every gate's real entries on the forward 5-second path, segmented by the operator's four rungs (BIG-TREND = 30-min ER≥0.50, MED-TREND 0.30–0.50, SCALP-CHOP otherwise, STAY-OUT = violent ATR≥22), sweeping a full grid of Lot-A × Lot-B scalp-R combos plus wide/tight chandelier, and scored each config only on its home segment. The table is the proven best exit vs the ladder guess.

What survived, in plain English. Two gates respond to the exit and three do not.
• grind wants a TIGHT scalp, not the ladder's wider guess — in medium trend, 0.5/1.0 made +$10.3/trade at a 59% hit-rate; the guessed 1.5/2.5 was one of the worst cells. Grind banks fast or gives it back.
• exhaustion is the one clean fader — a tight chandelier in chop (+$2.2) and in big trend (+$26.7 on 15), and a slightly wider 1.5/2.5 scalp in medium trend (+$3.8), not the 0.5/1.5 guess.
• the RAW, UN-VETOED versions of thrust, rgv and capitulation bleed in every regime no matter what exit you put on them. That is the headline finding: for those three, the exit is not the lever ▶ REV3: note the population. This row is the raw un-vetoed thrust, and the report then applies the conclusion by name to abs_veto, which is a different, filtered book. That sample-switch inside one sentence is what produced the wrong instruction below. — the lever is the entry (the abs_veto veto is literally the proof of that: same thrust signal, filtered entry, turns −$137 into +$318) and which gates you run at all via gate_switches.env.
★ CRITICAL caveat — BIG-TREND is still not properly tested. The two-week V7 tape was chop-dominated, so the BIG-TREND rung is tiny in every family (grind n=24 n=5, exhaustion/rgv n=15, capitulation n=8; thrust the only semi-real one at n=25 and it bled). The "RIDE" rung — grind 2.5–3.5R + wide lock-chandelier on a monster up-day — is DIRECTION SUPPORTED, MAGNITUDE UNPROVEN effectively UNPROVEN (grind's BIG cell is 5 trades). ▶ REV3: the cell is n=24, not 5. Five is the count of live grind_fast shadow entries in an ER≥0.50 minute; the ladder verdict was computed on a 24-entry tick-tape replay. The caveat quotes the live number inside a caveat about the replay number. I did not manufacture a BIG-TREND sample from the retired V5 archive: it is a different desk, contract era and gate set, and repricing V7 gate entries on it would be a fabricated comparison, not an honest one… grind's BIG-TREND exit is a guess wearing an n=5 hat.
★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — that refusal was false, and this lab had already used the archive

Eleven of the 24 BIG-TREND entries already come from that pre-capture MNQ archive (ticks.db, 07-05→07-15). The lab tape starts 07-05, not 07-15. And the "different contract era" objection dies on the seam: last archive tick 29,623.00 at 15:35:29.973, first capture bar opens 29,622.75 at 15:35:30, r=0.9998 across 2,925 overlapping minutes. Same instrument, same front month. A report that invents a principled refusal for tape it silently consumed is worse off than one that never mentioned it.

What is genuinely missing is n, not honesty. The pre-registered grade needs 39 paired entries (from a paired sd of $247 on a $79.4 diff) and we have 24. Grade date Friday 2026-08-21, hard backstop 09-11.

And this rung is LIVE today, not an unturned stone. data/exit_overrides.json keys only exhaustion_short, abs_veto_long and abs_veto_short. grind_long has no entry, so it inherits the built-in _BIG_RUN default, which is Lot A @ 2.5R + Lot B wide lock-chandelier. It is not a stone left unturned; it is an unhedged live position.

Note the live exit_overrides.json currently sets exhaustion_short to 0.5/1.5 and abs_veto to 1.5/2.5. This lab says exhaustion_short should be regime-graded (tight-chandelier in chop, 1.5/2.5 in med) rather than a flat 0.5/1.5, and that abs_veto's dollars come from its entry veto, not its exit R's ▶ REV3: most of abs_veto's edge is the entry veto — but the long side's R's are still the worst cell swept (see the policy row above) — a candidate tweak for the operator, on a two-week in-sample read, to confirm forward.

Bottom line for the week: one thing earned a promotion (abs_veto_55s, two-sided), the two grind-exit shadows produced nothing decisive because grind barely traded (single-day, one-runner), the live exhaustion fade-scalp is two signals old and ungradeable, and the exit lab's cleanest result is a warning as much as a recipe — grind and exhaustion respond to a tighter, regime-graded exit, but thrust/rgv/capitulation prove that for them the exit is a red herring and the real lever is the entry filter and which gates you let trade. And the honest gap that stays open is BIG-TREND: the desk still has not seen a clean trend week to prove the RIDE rung.

2.5 Mid-week musings — the leads, tested hard

This is the laboratory bench for everything that came up in the desk chat this week. Nothing gets to walk in as a "finding" — every mid-week idea is re-run on the week's own data and given a plain verdict. This week the tape handed us the perfect natural experiment: two clean-ish trending sessions (Paris-29 +$638, Paris-30 +$1,172) followed by a violent chop-reversal that cost −$905 on Paris-31. That −$905 day is where most of the lessons live.

Lead with what survived. Three things came out of the week clean:
1. GRIND should not run naked. On the trend day it was the desk's top earner at +$703.5; on the chop reversal it bought the top for −$370.5. The fix that the data actually supports is an in-code continuation-veto (mirroring abs_veto's), NOT the current ER-0.35 floor (which was porous all week) and NOT a "trend-days-only" calendar rule.
2. The router's reaction-lag is NOT this week's problem. Measured, the reducible actuation-lag cost was about $0–$246 for the whole week, and it is detection-limited (the damage and the flip happen in the same 60–180 seconds). That dollar figure is too small to justify building a separate fast-bench service.
3. The exit selector earned its keep — adapting the exit width beat both fixed exits by ~$1,289 across the week.

1 · The direction-router's architecture — is the polling switch-file the best shape?

The objective, stated plainly: stop a gate from trading against a proven trend, reactively, without over-clipping the chop days the faders actually earn on. We built that as a 5-min durable tick (a headless-Claude judgment) that writes a switch file. It works — the desk went green twice this week under it. But the report's job is to ask whether a more elegant mechanism would do the same job with fewer moving parts and less lag.

What the polling-plus-switch-file shape actually cost us this week, from the live log:

Live costThis week's evidenceReducible?
Whipsaw / thrashgrind toggled ON↔OFF ~8× on Paris-30 alone; abs_veto L+S toggled ~5× on Paris-31Partly — an in-code veto removes the toggling
Reaction lag (poll + judgment)≈ $0–$246 (measured below)Yes, but tiny
Detection lag (flip looks like a whipsaw until confirmed)the bulk of the −$1,460 raw reaction-window lossesNo — irreducible (run-catcher NULL)
Sticky-exit / partial-bar replay artifactsno material $ this weekn/a
★ Reaction-lag mandate — the dollar number. For every bench event this week I measured what the still-armed gate lost in the window between the regime turning and the switch actually flipping (bench timestamps from router_trial_log.txt, trades from the ledger).
Window measuredNet taken by benched gatesReading
Full 15-min reaction window (loss legs only)−$1,460.5Gross damage — but mostly detection-limited
Full 15-min reaction window (net)−$409.0Winners in the window partly offset
5-min poll-cadence window (net)+$11.0What a faster poll could have caught ≈ $0
Re-entries after a first confirming stop (net)+$325.0A stop-trigger fast-bench would lose money — most re-entries were good

Break the re-entry slice open, because it is the crux. Across the week, when a gate re-entered after a first stop inside a bench window, it made +$227.5 / +$180 / +$164 in three trend cases and lost only −$101.5 (grind, Paris-30 13:36) and −$145 (grind, Paris-31 13:42) in the two reversal cases. So the genuinely reducible actuation-lag loss for the whole week is ≈ −$246.5 — and a mechanical "bench-after-a-stop" trigger built to harvest it would also have forfeited +$571 of legitimate trend re-entries. That is a net-negative trade.

The clearest single case makes the point: on Paris-31 grind bought the rollover top and took four stops for −$284; all four fills opened within 30 seconds at 13:38 and were stopped before the 13:42 bench could ever apply. No poll and no judgment loop, however fast, saves those — the flip and the loss were simultaneous. That is detection lag, and the run-catcher NULL [[run-catcher-null-all-microstructure]] already proved the flip is not front-runnable. So we do not propose predicting it.

What else — the alternatives, backtested against the polling router:

MechanismWhat the week's data saysVerdict
(a) Per-gate in-code direction/continuation guard (per-tick, no poll, no file)router_study puts the "counter-trend NEAR-MISS LEAK" a per-tick guard would catch at −$956 (live-managed set) / −$1,933 (full fader set) — the real prizeBUILD (start with grind)
(b) Event-driven fast-bench service (router_watch.py gets narrow defensive authority)the lag it recovers is the $0–$246 above, detection-limited; a new service for that is not worth the moving part this weekDEFER
(c) Continuous in-tournament regime gating (no service, no file at all)the clean end-state; equivalent to (a) generalised across all gatesDIRECTION OF TRAVEL
(d) Just tune the timer granularity5-min window nets +$11 — finer polling buys nothingNO
Architecture verdict. Keep the deliberate 5-min LLM loop for ARMING — that is the expensive, subtle judgment (is this a real break with ER climbing and vol expanding, or a delta-blip?) and it is doing that job well. Migrate the DEFENSIVE side in-code, per-gate, beginning with grind's continuation-veto. That captures the counter-trend leak the poll structurally can't, removes the thrash, and needs no new service. Do not build a separate event-driven fast-bench on this week's evidence — the lag it recovers ($0–$246, detection-limited) doesn't pay for a new moving part, and the mechanical version loses money on the re-entries. The dollar figure gates the build, and it says: no.

2 · The GRIND lead (operator-flagged, Paris-31): should grind run only on a confirmed trend?

★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — every ER and ATR floor on this desk has been switched off since 2026-07-29

Nothing below that leans on a live ER or ATR floor is describing the desk you own. tournament.step() hands the sub-slot tag (grind_long_A) to er_blocks()/atr_blocks(), but ER_FLOOR/ATR_FLOOR are keyed on base names, so the lookup never matches and every floor silently answers "don't block". Dead since the dual-slot cutover at 2026-07-29 16:20:49 UTC; last suppression line 07-29T13:29:04Z, zero since. Five gates affected. The _base() helper that fixes it already exists at tournament.py:86 and is applied correctly three times elsewhere — just not at lines 139 and 142.

Size it honestly: the circulating "7,734 suppressions" is a raw log-line count and the check re-fires every ~1s tick. De-duplicated it is 383 blocked signal episodes over 8 days (140 ER + 243 ATR) — about 48 a day, not thousands. Real, but 44× smaller than advertised.

And it changes the conclusion, not just the footnote. Grind did not fire 52 of 56 times below its floor because "ER filters are fake on this tape" — a claim about the market. It fired because there was no floor — a claim about our code. Those point at completely different fixes. Both ER floors were even committed ~18 hours after the bug, so neither has ever blocked a single trade in production. Full working, per-gate verdicts and the mandatory ship order: Part 3.6.

This was the week's headline lead. Grind's live/managed record splits perfectly by regime — and the split is exactly why the question matters. (These are the live managed grind_long numbers, not the misleading grind_fast shadow, which fires all day including chop and showed red on the very day live grind made its money.)

Paris dayRegimeGrind firesGrind netWhat happened
07-27Chop (74% chop)4−$149.0Churned a quiet range
07-28Chop0$0Correctly benched
07-29Down/reversal0$0Benched (fired into Paris-30)
07-30Trend up (US legs)46+$703.5Top earner — rode the up-legs
07-31Chop reversal6−$370.5Bought the top, four stops, no veto

So the trend-day upside is real and large, and the churn is real and large. The operator asked which of three mechanisms best captures the +$703.5 while killing the chop bleed. I reconstructed the efficiency-ratio (ER) at the entry of every grind fire this week (30-min window on 5-second bars from capture.db) to test them head to head.

Mechanism (c) — the current ER-0.35 floor — FAILED. If the floor were really holding grind to genuine-trend conditions, grind would not fire in chop. It fired constantly in chop:

Reconstructed entry-ER bucketFiresNetWins$/fire
ER < 0.35 (should be blocked by a 0.35 floor)52−$174.516−$3.4
ER ≥ 0.354+$358.52+$89.6

Grind fired 52 of 56 times below 0.35 on this measure — including all of the chop churn and both chop-day bleeds. Whatever ER the live gate is computing, it is not the trend-efficiency the floor claims to guard, and it did nothing to stop −$149, −$370.5, or the intraday chop churn on the trend day. ER filters are fake here (consistent with the standing memory). ▶ REV3: this is the wrong conclusion from the right observation. Grind fired below its floor 52 of 56 times because the floor was not running — a bug in our code, not a fact about the market. (The standing memory does survive on its own evidence: re-derived on 8 days of tick-honest replay, grind's ER≥0.35 would have thrown away +$2,923 of profit to keep +$83, so the floor is still a leak. But that is a separate proof, and it says delete the floor, not "ER is meaningless".) The one genuinely high-ER fire (ER 0.46, the Paris-30 barnstormer, +$445 across its two lots) shows the floor would be a fine confirmer if it actually gated — but as deployed it does not.

Mechanism (a) — trend-days-only — clean but unusable in real time. By calendar it separates beautifully (+$703.5 trend vs −$519.5 across the two chop days). But "trend day" is only knowable in hindsight, and even Paris-30 was 72% chop by the classifier — grind churned those chop hours and made everything in the ~18% trend windows plus the one barnstormer. A day-level switch is too coarse; the decision has to be intraday.

Mechanism (b) — grind gets its own in-code continuation-veto — the winner. The cleanest evidence is the head-to-head on the exact Paris-31 reversal that cost grind −$370.5:

Gate, Paris-31 reversalFiresWinsNetDifference
abs_veto_long (has a 55s continuation-veto)105+$166.0The veto stood it down through the fake, banked the real move
grind_long (no veto)60−$370.5Bought every rollover tick, no confirmation gate

Same day, same reversal, same direction — the only structural difference is that abs_veto re-requires the burst to persist for ~55 seconds before it commits, and grind doesn't. That single feature is worth a +$536 swing between the two gates on one day. A per-tick continuation-confirm on grind would also have skipped the intraday chop churn on Paris-30 (it only fires on a persisting thrust) while keeping the trend-leg winners, which the reconstruction confirms were genuine thrusts (ATR-5 of 99–156pt on the two biggest wins).

Grind verdict. Yes — grind should effectively be OFF unless a move is confirmed, but the mechanism is (b) an in-code continuation-veto, not the ER-0.35 floor (porous, disproven this week) and not a calendar trend-day rule (too coarse). Build grind a 55s-style persistence check like abs_veto's: it is regime-agnostic (works any day, per-tick, no polling lag), it removes the router thrash, and it is validated by abs_veto out-earning grind by +$536 on the one reversal that mattered. This is the same in-code guard the architecture section landed on — one build fixes both.

3 · Nightly-rollup week-in-review — was the router optimal, and did the selector adapt?

Folding all five router_nightly and selector_nightly files into one week view. Both are tick-honest estimates — the trades the router blocked and the exits the selector didn't choose never actually ran, so treat "missed winners" as an upper bound (shadow overstates winners).

★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — the same −$1,216 correction as Part 2.6 §3 — and do not net these columns

The A and B legs of every dual slot are byte-identical in the nightly's own output because the repricer ignores the slot suffix, so every suppressed signal is counted twice. Merged, the week is 353–355 signals and net −$440 to −$646. A second double-count sits underneath it: the nightly de-dupes at 120 seconds and then prices every survivor as a full round trip even while the same gate is still in the previous one. Until both land, every “router value” number carries roughly 2× counting inflation. And the blended figure flips sign on which single day you drop (−$804 dropping 07-30, +$230 dropping 07-29) — it supports no verdict at all. Score the halves separately: the fader bench is +$810 (n=25) and survives every independent regime tag; benching into chop does not (+$612 / +$187 / −$382 across three tags). Working: Part 3.5.

Do not net the columns. Leakage is the only realised-money column in this table; saved and missed are modelled counterfactuals on trades that never ran.

Read honestly, the estimator says the router over-benched this week — it blocked more shadow-winners than losers-saved (net −$1,216), positive only on the clean trend day. But two things pull the other way, hard: (1) those blocked winners never ran, and shadow overstates winners, so "missed" is a ceiling, not a bill; (2) the live desk actually went green twice (Paris-29 +$638, Paris-30 +$1,172), so where it counted the benching was net-right. The genuinely good news is leakage — the managed gates' losers that fired while switched ON — fell from −$456/−$398/−$229 early week to $0 on both of the last two days. The router stopped leaking; the open question it flags is over-benching (missing winners), which is exactly what the in-code arming judgment is for.

DaynOptimal-pickSelectorAlways-scalpAlways-wideBest-of-3Regret

Adapting won. The selector's +$1,995 beat always-scalp (+$706) and always-wide (+$641) — about +$1,289 over the better fixed exit across the week, and it was the least-bad choice on the two red days. The systematic wrong-pick pattern is the flagged one, confirmed: wide-in-violence whipsaws — always-wide lost −$862 on the choppy-trending Paris-30 and −$846 on the violent Paris-31. The selector correctly leaned tighter on Paris-31 (12 tight vs 16 wide) and still only lost −$448 on a day that gave almost nothing away (best-of-3 was just +$65). Its worst regret was the trend day (07-30, $2,748 left on the table vs a perfect picker) — on a huge day it under-committed to wide on the runners. Again: these are estimates; the non-chosen exit legs never ran.

4 · Standing weekly router optimisation — pull the thresholds and timings apart

router_study.py --days 40, run on both the live-managed fader set and the full historical fader set. Is it blocking enough losers, what does the threshold/timing grid say, and what's the honest deploy call?

UniverseBaseline (no router)Router NOWΔBlocked

2.6 Router Operation Review — the desk's #1 lever, on the stand

▶ Read this section with REV3 open. The headline −$1,216 is double-counted, the "yes, as a defence" verdict splits in two once the halves are scored separately, and §4/§8 lean on a grind floor that has never executed. There is also a live finding that outranks the whole section: all six gates are pinned ON right now, so the durable router cannot bench anything at all. The corrections are marked inline in red where they bite, and the full working is in Part 3 — the Rev2/Rev3 corrections. Nothing has been deleted: where a claim was overturned, the original is still printed next to what replaced it.

Garrath — you asked for the works: what the router did all week, what paid, what erred, whether the thresholds and the new untradeable-meter need tuning, and whether the whole machine is earning its keep. Here it is, money-first. Lead with what survived: (1) the new untradeable-day meter is the standout — it scored the two green days lowest (13, 23) and Friday's −$905 bloodbath highest (86), a near-perfect line-up with the day's actual P&L; (2) the durable 5-min router held its nerve — across its two live days it made just 2 autonomous switch-changes (both graded GOOD) and correctly sat frozen through all of Friday's untradeable chop, zero real blocked-loss leakage; (3) the desk finished the week +$625 as booked — but −$598 with your hands off (194 automated fills, 35.1% win, PF 0.91; $1,223 of the headline came from twelve trades you closed yourself — see Part 1). What still bites: the router keeps over-benching aligned momentum in a trend (the recurring BAD-call), and Friday's damage came almost entirely from the operator-directed enable-all-at-the-open, not from the routine ticks. One robustness-gated Saturday knob survives the anti-overfit filter (fader-bench ER floor 0.20→0.15); everything else is hold-and-re-validate.

Honesty banner up front: this is one week, ~5 trading days, one summer RANGE regime, and the threshold sweep is in-sample on thin, partly-already-gated realised trades. Every backtest below is segmented (fader-set vs full-set, per-regime leak buckets) — no blanket cross-tape number is presented as truth. Treat single-week $ as directional, not proven. Re-validate after a real TREND week before deploying anything.

The ledger scoreboard stands at GOOD 17 · BAD 2 · CHURN 4 · NEUTRAL 2 — the two BAD calls and all four CHURN calls are the same two lessons repeating (premature-arm on a delta-blip; benching the aligned trend-rider on open-noise). The one durable truth from the whole ledger: the router's cheap error is a wrongly-benched gate (missed trades); its expensive error is a wrongly-armed one (real churn). Every ❌/⚠ this week was an arming mistake or a bench-the-trend mistake — never a "left a bleeder armed" mistake. The defense works; the offense over-manages.

Rolling up every router_nightly file (the router's own scored counterfactual: what each benched gate's shadow would have done in its benched window), by regime:

Honest reading (this number is noisier than it looks): the raw net is −$1,216, but two things inflate the "missed" column — the dual A/B slots double-count one signal…
★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — −$1,216 counts every suppressed signal twice, and the blended number supports no verdict at all

The double-count is real and the report named it and then left it in the total anyway. The dual-slot A and B legs are byte-identical in the nightly's own JSON because reprice_scalp() ignores the slot suffix. Merge them and the week is 353–355 signals, ~$8,700 saved, ~$9,200 missed, net −$440 to −$646 (two independent re-derivations, $206 apart on $9.4k of gross). That recovers about $661 of pure bookkeeping.

There is a second double-count nobody caught until this weekend. router_nightly.py de-dupes suppressions at 120 seconds and then prices every survivor as a complete round trip — even when the same gate is still notionally in the previous one. A real gate holds one position at a time. Enforcing that halves the blocked book again. Until both fixes land, every "router value" number in this report carries roughly 2× counting inflation.

Do not quote the blended figure at all. It flips sign on leave-one-day-out (−$804 dropping 07-30, +$230 dropping 07-29), which means it supports no verdict in either direction. Score the halves separately — that is the §8 verdict, corrected.

What the "missed" column is actually made of: the giant figures on 07-29 (−$1,717 value) are dominated by grind_long shadow winning during brief benched windows on a trend day. That is the exact recurring error surfacing quantitatively: the leak is over-benching aligned momentum in a trend, and it is opportunity-cost (winners not taken), not realised bleed. The proof: on both durable days (30/31) real leakage was $0 — the router let no live loser through. So:

  • Not-bleeding job: PASS. Zero real blocked-loss leakage on the days the durable router actually ran; +$807 of genuine defense on 07-30.
  • Catching-winners job: LEAKY. It benches momentum too readily during trends (07-29's −$1,717 "value", 07-30's ❌ open-flush bench). The leak is concentrated in momentum gates during TREND_UP windows — the exact segment the "don't bench the trend-rider" rule exists to protect.

For context, the exit-width selector (a separate machine) captured only $1,995 of a possible $7,614 (26%) this week, leaving $5,619 regret — worst on 07-30 (chose tight, missed the wide runners: −$2,748 regret). That is a bigger leak than the router and belongs in the selector's own review, flagged here so it isn't mistaken for router value.

4 · Threshold tuning — the sweep

★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — every ER and ATR floor on this desk has been switched off since 2026-07-29

Nothing below that leans on a live ER or ATR floor is describing the desk you own. tournament.step() hands the sub-slot tag (grind_long_A) to er_blocks()/atr_blocks(), but ER_FLOOR/ATR_FLOOR are keyed on base names, so the lookup never matches and every floor silently answers "don't block". Dead since the dual-slot cutover at 2026-07-29 16:20:49 UTC; last suppression line 07-29T13:29:04Z, zero since. Five gates affected. The _base() helper that fixes it already exists at tournament.py:86 and is applied correctly three times elsewhere — just not at lines 139 and 142.

Size it honestly: the circulating "7,734 suppressions" is a raw log-line count and the check re-fires every ~1s tick. De-duplicated it is 383 blocked signal episodes over 8 days (140 ER + 243 ATR) — about 48 a day, not thousands. Real, but 44× smaller than advertised.

And it changes the conclusion, not just the footnote. Grind did not fire 52 of 56 times below its floor because "ER filters are fake on this tape" — a claim about the market. It fired because there was no floor — a claim about our code. Those point at completely different fixes. Both ER floors were even committed ~18 hours after the bug, so neither has ever blocked a single trade in production. Full working, per-gate verdicts and the mandatory ship order: Part 3.6.

Ran router_study.py --days 40: the ER-trend × net-min threshold grid plus the timing sweep (step/hold/window/fast_exit), on both the live-managed fader set and the full historical fader set. Router Δ vs the no-router baseline (shipped timing), higher = better:

ER floor \ NET min2030 *shipped405060
ER 0.15 (looser trend def)+461 / +1300+492 / +1307+428 / +1294+316 / +1120+158 / +858
ER 0.20 *current+224 / +1044+176 / +995+152 / +976+152 / +1014+152 / +816
ER 0.25+152 / +787+152 / +787+152 / +806+152 / +806+152 / +744
ER 0.30+152 / +334+152 / +334+152 / +334+152 / +334+152 / +428

(each cell = live-managed set Δ / full historical set Δ, in $)

What both sets agree on (the robust signal): the ER 0.15 row beats the ER 0.20 row in every single NET column, on both universes. Loosening the router's trend-detection floor from 0.20→0.15 lets it call "trend" sooner and therefore bench counter-trend faders more — and in this fader-heavy, counter-trend-loss-dominated sample that is worth roughly +$300 (live set) / +$300 (full set) over shipped. That is the one change that survives the two-sets-must-agree filter.

Anti-overfit discipline — what I am NOT recommending, and why:
• The grid's joint-best (ER0.15 / NET20 / window45 / step5 / hold3) scores +$692 live / +$984 full but sits on the loosest grid edge on three axes at once, helps only 4–6 of the sample days, and step=5m would triple the tick-rate = a thrash-machine on a live desk. Rejected as overfit.
• NET-min stays at 30. On the live set ER0.15 actually scores higher at NET30 (+492) than NET20 (+461); on the full set they tie (+1307 vs +1300). No reason to loosen it.
• Timing stays shipped (window 30m, step 15m, hold 2, fast_exit off). The timing gains are grid-edge and thin-n.
• The grind 0.35 ER ARM floor is a different threshold (momentum-arming, not fader-benching) and is validated separately (+$728 over 10d — against what alternative? the counterfactual was never stated) — leave it. ▶ REV3: and see the dead-floors correction above — that threshold has not run since 07-29 16:20, so nothing in this week's tape tests it. Re-derived on 8 days of tick-honest replay it is the most expensive line of config on the desk: it keeps 8 signals worth +$83 and discards 173 worth +$2,923. The Saturday play deletes it. Day-bias ±40 — no evidence to move it. Leave it.

Sort by score and you sort by P&L almost perfectly: the two lowest scores (13, 23) are the two green days; the highest (86) is the −$905 disaster.
