One floor out of five passes. Both bases agree on every verdict — no call in this table depends on which price stream you score it with.

The standing priors, tested rather than inherited. Memory says "ER filters are FAKE, ATR floors are REAL", and separately that abs_veto wants no ER floor while grind wanted 0.35.

  • "ER filters are FAKE" — CONFIRMED, emphatically. Both live ER floors block robustly profitable books on both price bases and all eight LODO folds. The abs_veto_long blocked book is positive on every single one of the 8 folds (+$4.88 to +$24.27 per signal).
  • "abs_veto wants NO ER floor" — CONFIRMED, and now we know why. Segmented, the entire blocked book is NORMAL-CHOP: 40 signals, +$680. The ER floor is precisely an anti-home filter — it fires exactly where Q2 showed abs_veto lives. It also makes the gate less robust: break-even loss multiplier falls from 1.27 to 1.13 with the floor on.
  • "grind wanted 0.35" — REFUTED. This is the most expensive single line of config on the desk. It keeps 8 signals worth +$83 and discards 173 worth +$2,923.
  • "ATR floors are REAL" — HALF TRUE, and the half matters. ATR floors do sharpen per-trade quality monotonically (grind's kept book goes $16.60 → $67 → $111 per signal as the floor climbs from 0 to 30). But total dollars peak far lower than the live settings. The floors are real; the values are roughly double where the money is.

4 · Per-gate detail — the number behind each verdict

grind_long ATR: 24 → 10 (a genuine plateau at 9–11)

Swept fine, under the ext_hi 2.0 ceiling. The blocked book is the test:

ATR floorblocked nblocked netblk strip-3blk LODOkept nkept netkept $/sig
83−$13——108+$2,944$27.26
99−$144−$222−$25.44102+$3,074$30.14
10 ← re-derived11−$165−$293−$22.42100+$3,095$30.95
1116−$3−$290−$16.9595+$2,934$30.88
12 ← the report's rec27+$776+$157+$14.8084+$2,154$25.64
24 ← LIVE as written91+$1,712+$742+$15.4320+$1,218$60.90

9, 10 and 11 all block a robustly negative book (negative net, negative strip-3, negative LODO) and sit within $161 of each other on kept net — a real plateau, not a spike. At 12 the blocked book flips sharply positive and stays positive all the way up. The report's recommended 12 is one notch the wrong side of the cliff and costs $941 versus 10. The plateau holds without the ceiling too (at ATR≥10, no ceiling: blocked 19 signals −$134, strip-3 −$376, LODO −$10.74), so this value does not depend on shipping the ceiling.

abs_veto_short ATR ≥ 16 — the one honest coin-flip

Blocked book is net positive (+$137 tick / +$256 quote) so it fails the stated test — but that positive is entirely tail-driven: strip its 3 best trades and it is −$197, and the worst LODO fold is −$11.02. The IS/OOS halves flip sign (+$417 then −$280). Meanwhile the floor does improve resilience: break-even multiplier 1.36 → 1.45, per-signal $15.07 → $22.02. This floor is genuinely unproven in both directions. Per the standing rule — a floor that cannot be shown to earn its keep is deleted, not tuned — I recommend deleting it, but it is the one line in this report where your own risk appetite legitimately overrides the evidence, and the cost of being wrong either way is about ±$150 over eight days. Separately, a lead, not a recommendation: an ER≥0.10 floor on this gate blocks 14 signals worth −$183 (strip-3 −$605, LODO −$26.00) and lifts break-even to 1.54. That is a better filter than the ATR one — but it is a new filter and belongs in shadow first, not in tonight's window.

capitulation_long ATR ≥ 10 — KEEP the floor, but the floor is not the problem

The only floor that passes cleanly, on both bases and every robustness test. Keep it at 10. But be clear about what it is buying: the gate loses money at every floor value — −$1,202 with no floor, −$743 at ATR 10, −$540 at ATR 14, and it only stops losing when the floor is high enough that it stops trading. Break-even loss multiplier 0.41. The floor is harm-reduction on a gate that needs a relegation conversation, not a tuning one; its live scale-out record agrees (5 signals, −$128.50). Lead only, on the loose proxy population: an ER≥0.25 floor flips it to +$124 on n=13, break-even 1.48. Too thin and on the wrong population to act on tonight.

5 · ★ The interaction that gates tonight — grind_long

Three questions, answered directly.

(a) Was the grind replay applying the floors as config, or did it inherit the dead-floor reality? It applied them as config. scripts/rehab_grind_long.py:124 reads if er < er_floor or f.atr < atr_floor: continue, and line 234 hard-codes [x for x in raw if x["er"] >= 0.35 and x["atr"] >= 24.0]. The funnel re-runs as: 1,232 raw signals → 93 clear ER 0.35 → 171 clear ATR 24 → 25 clear both → 11 realised trades. So the "11 fires −$284" baseline is a simulation of a configuration that was not running. The honest before/after is +$1,552 → +$2,830, not −$284 → +$2,830. Roughly 59% of the advertised delta is the removal of filters that were already de-facto removed. The report is internally aware of this — Q2 §3 even says Part 1.5 needs re-reading — but the numbers in part1_5_rehab.html, rehab_grind_long.md and the money_gbp: 700 in plays.json were never corrected.

(b) If the wiring fix ships, do grind's floors come back to life and silently undo the entry fix? Yes — catastrophically, and it would not be silent for long.

worldnnet$/sigwin%strip-3LODO min@1.5× lossesbreak-even ×

World B is the answer to (b): 181 signals collapse to 4, and +$3,005 becomes +$360. On the quote basis it is 156 → 3. Ninety-eight percent of grind's fires disappear. That is not "the entry fix gets undone" — it is "the gate gets switched off by accident".

The ceiling itself is real and it is the only genuinely new lever here. I reproduced the report's headline independently: filtering to ext_atr ≤ 2.0 gives 111 fires / +$2,930 against their 111 fires / +$2,830 — a 3.5% agreement from a completely separate code path, which is strong corroboration of both harnesses. The plateau across ext_hi 1.5 / 1.75 / 2.0 is broad ($2,646 / $2,931 / $2,930). And the honest argument for the ceiling is not the raw dollars — those barely move — it is resilience: at 1.5× losses the uncapped book is −$1,449 while the capped book is +$358. The ceiling does not make more money; it makes the same money out of 39% fewer trades, which is what survives contact with real fills.

Row E beats row D on every measure — net (+$941), per-signal, win rate, strip-best-3 (+$1,326 vs +$385), worst LODO fold (+$23.58 vs +$14.36), the 1.5× stress test (+$669 vs −$98) and break-even multiplier (1.64 vs 1.48) — and on both IS and OOS halves. All 8 LODO folds of row E are positive, ranging $23.58 to $37.81 per signal. Ship ATR 10, not 12.

(c) The desk-wide cost of getting the order wrong. Shipping D1 alone, across all four floored gates, on 8 days of tick-honest replay:

6 · The ship order for tonight — concrete, ordered, revertible

The one rule that makes this safe. Because the floors are currently dead, changing the floor VALUES today is provably a no-op on the live desk. That is the whole trick: land the correct values first while they cannot possibly do harm, then flip the wiring as a single, atomic, one-line arming moment. Never ship the wiring fix before or without the values. If you only remember one thing, remember that tournament.py:139/142 is the master switch and it goes last.

STEP 1 — land the re-derived floor values (no-op by construction). src/gazbot7/deciders.py:

-ER_FLOOR = {"abs_veto_long": 0.25, "grind_long": 0.35}
+ER_FLOOR: dict = {}   # ★2026-08-01 REV3: both momentum-long ER floors DELETED. Re-derived on 8d
+                      # tick-honest replay: grind 0.35 blocked +$2,923 (strip-3 +$1,132, all 8 LODO
+                      # folds positive); abs_veto_long 0.25 blocked +$640, all of it NORMAL-CHOP,
+                      # the gate's home cell. Never ran live (committed after the 07-29 cutover).

-ATR_FLOOR = {"grind_long": 24.0, "capitulation_long": 10.0, "abs_veto_short": 16.0}
+ATR_FLOOR = {"grind_long": 10.0, "capitulation_long": 10.0}   # ★2026-08-01 REV3: grind 24→10
+          # (plateau 9-11; at 12+ the blocked book flips positive). capitulation 10 KEPT — the only
+          # floor whose blocked book is robustly negative. abs_veto_short 16 DELETED — unproven.

Verify: restart, then confirm the journal still shows zero ER gate: / ATR gate: lines. If any appear, something else changed — stop. Revert: restore the two literals; no-op either way while Step 2 is unshipped.

STEP 2 — the wiring fix. This is the arming moment. src/gazbot7/tournament.py:

-            if er_blocks(slot, er):
+            if er_blocks(_base(slot), er):
-            if atr_blocks(slot, f.atr):
+            if atr_blocks(_base(slot), f.atr):

Optionally also :148/:154 slot → _base(slot) to revive the ER-hold shadow study (observe-only, no P&L effect). Verify within one session: suppression lines reappear with base names; grind_long ATR blocks should run at roughly 10% of fires and capitulation_long at 43%; there should be zero ER gate: lines anywhere (both ER floors are now empty). Any ER gate: line means Step 1 did not take. Revert: one-line, _base(slot) → slot. This single revert neutralises Steps 1 and 2 together and returns the desk to exactly today's behaviour — it is the kill switch.

STEP 3 — the grind ext_hi ceiling. Separate commit, separate restart, separate observation. src/gazbot7/slot_strategy.py:118:

-                 params={"slope_min": 0.4, "fast_slope": True}, sizing="conviction", base_size=2,
+                 params={"slope_min": 0.4, "fast_slope": True, "ext_hi": 2.0}, sizing="conviction", base_size=2,

This is the only change here that alters entry behaviour immediately and unconditionally — it is not gated by the wiring bug, so it has real effect the moment the service restarts. Expect roughly 39% fewer grind fires. The mechanism already exists (gate_grind(..., ext_hi=4.0) at deciders.py:370, enforced at :380); only the value is being set. Revert: delete "ext_hi": 2.0 from the dict → back to the 4.0 default. Keep this out of the Step-2 commit so that if grind misbehaves you can tell which change did it.

STEP 4 — nothing else tonight. The abs_veto_short ER≥0.10 lead and the capitulation_long ER≥0.25 lead are both interesting and both go to shadow, not to live. Neither is in the diff above.

Router benching is untouched by all of this and remains reversible every 5 minutes.

7 · The biggest risk, and what this does NOT prove

The biggest risk if you ship all three tonight is that grind_long is a high-count, low-margin book being asked to survive real fills. Even the best config — row E — has a break-even loss multiplier of 1.64, and memory [[shadow-sim-understates-losses]] puts live losses at 1.1–3.1× modelled. That number sits inside the band. If live losses on this gate run at 1.7× rather than 1.5×, the entire +$3,095 is zero, and everything in §5 becomes an argument about noise. The ceiling helps (it is why row E survives 1.5× where row A does not) but it does not lift grind out of the band. Grind is being made less bad and more robust, not made good.

Behind that, three more:

  • My replay is always-armed; the live desk is not. In the same window the router benched 465 grind/abs_veto signal episodes. The interaction between the router's benching and the new floors is unmeasured — the two filters could stack and starve the gate, or overlap and do nothing. Watch the combined block rate on the first session, not just the floor's.
  • Eight days, one regime. Chop-dominated, MNQ-only, PAPER. CLEAN-TREND is n=1 for grind at the ceiling — completely unscored. A wide ext_atr is exactly what a trend day pays for, and ext_hi 2.0 caps it. If Monday opens trending, Step 3 is the first thing to question.
  • Two population caveats. capitulation_long's proxy runs require_flip=False against the live True, so its rows are directional only. And abs_veto_short's ATR verdict is a coin-flip I have called by rule rather than by evidence — I would not defend it hard either way.

What would change my mind: for grind, one clean trend week with ext_atr above 2.0 paying — that is the single scenario this tape cannot speak to. For the two ER deletions, nothing on this evidence; they are the most robust calls in the report, positive on both price bases and all eight folds.

REV3 grind_long's exit, swept properly — and what the cell should be

PATCH MARKER — this section closes the hole REV2 · Q1 flagged in itself ("grind_long's 2.5/wide pair was not put through this ladder … that sweep is the obvious next job and its absence is a hole in this section, not a silence"). It also patches PART 2 · LAB's grind rows and refutes REV2 · Q0 item M5 (grind_long → {a_r 0.5, b 1.0}), and it extends REV2 · Q3 by grading its pre-registered freeze cell on tick data a fortnight early. Where this section and any of those disagree, this section is the correction.

Garrath — the short version is that we were arguing about the wrong knob. I swept grind_long's exit across 130 configurations on 3,064 tick-repriced entries over 16 sessions, and not one exit setting makes the gate green. The loaded default (Lot A 2.5R + Lot B wide lock-chandelier) loses −$2,115 over those 16 sessions; the single best cell on the board still loses −$414. What the sweep does settle is the direction of travel, and it is the opposite of what the Friday report recommended: tighter is worse, not better. The M5 proposal (0.5R / 1.0R) is −$1,127 worse than what is loaded today and fails every robustness test I ran. My recommendation is the small, pre-registered move: write {"grind_long": {"a_r": 3.0, "b": "wide"}} — Lot A from 2.5R to 3.0R, Lot B unchanged — worth +$617 over the 16 sessions against the loaded baseline. And then stop tuning the exit, because the same data says the arming decision is worth three to five times as much.

The one-paragraph answer

Recommended cell: {"grind_long": {"a_r": 3.0, "b": "wide"}}. Baseline: the built-in BIG-RUN default it inherits today, Lot A 2.5R + Lot B wide lock-chandelier, which scores −$2,115 over 16 tick-covered sessions (−$1.72 per lot-fill, 26% win). The recommended cell scores −$1,498 (−$1.31 per lot-fill) — a delta of +$617, about +$39 a session at sim fill-density, nearer +$10 a session at the live desk's actual fill rate. It passes leave-one-day-out (worst-case diff still +$222), beats the default on 10 of 16 sessions, and sits on the correct side of the one finding that survives every test: the 3.5R rung beats both of the loaded legs in both halves of the tape (+$997 archive, +$705 capture). It fails the bootstrap bar (P(diff>0) = 0.82, not 0.95), fails strip-the-best-3-days (−$130), and fails the out-of-sample sign test (archive half −$76 against capture half +$693). So it is a sensible small move on a proven direction, not a proven number — which is exactly why I am recommending the cell REV2·Q3 already pre-registered for Monday rather than the cell with the prettiest statistics (3.5R/3.5R, +$1,702, passes everything except the plateau test, and it is a one-rung spike). The single biggest caveat: none of this makes grind_long profitable. It is red at every setting, before any slippage.

Fact table 2 — how this was measured, and the one methodological change that mattered

Two simulators, same tape, same ticks. The difference between them is the whole reason the Friday lab and this section disagree.

MethodWhat it doessignalsVerdict on it
Paired (the Friday lab's method)take every gate signal as an independent entry, reprice all exits on the same forward path, average902good for mechanism questions (entry variance cancels) — but it stacks overlapping entries: on a real run the gate fires every few minutes and the sim buys all of them, so trend tape gets counted 10–20 times over. Its answer for the loaded default was +$2,961 — a number the live desk has never seen.
Sequential (headline here)each lot is a slot holding one position; it takes the next signal only once it is flat again — exactly what the live dual-slot desk does3,064the honest policy test. Same default scores −$2,115, which is the sign and the shape the live ledger shows. Every headline number below is sequential.

The plateau test, stated plainly

There is no plateau at the winner. 3.5R beats its neighbours by $353 and $709 on a 16-session sample where session-to-session swings are ±$1,000 — that is inside the noise, and the desk's own rule is that a spike is not a result. What is a plateau is the region: on the full tape every fixed target from 3.0R to 6.0R sits between −$207 and −$985, every target at 2.5R or tighter sits between −$1,000 and −$1,915, and all three tightening chandeliers sit below −$1,900. The shape is proven; the point is not.

And the shape is weaker than that sounds, so here is the half-split before anyone over-reads it. The wide-beats-tight ordering does not hold rung-by-rung inside each half: in the 10 archive sessions the least-bad single lot is actually 0.5R (−$1,227, against −$1,264 for 3.5R and −$1,901 for the wide chandelier), and in the 6 capture sessions 1.5R (+$1,071) edges 3.5R (+$1,058). What survives the split is narrower and is the thing I am relying on: 3.5R beats both of the currently-loaded legs — 2.5R and the wide chandelier — in the archive half and in the capture half. Everything wider than that (4R, 5R, 6R) is worse than 3.5R in both halves too. So "go a bit wider on Lot A, leave Lot B alone" is supported on both halves; "wide is always better than tight" is not, and I am not claiming it.

scalp 1.5R−2,071+1,071−1,000

Note the honest wrinkle in the recommended cell: 3.0R is worse than the loaded 2.5R in the archive half (−$1,700 vs −$1,624) and much better in the capture half (+$1,140 vs +$446). That is precisely why its bootstrap comes out at 0.82 and not 0.95, and why I am calling +$617 a direction rather than a forecast.

The per-fill trap. M5's 0.5/1.0 cell loses less per fill than the loaded default (−$1.09 vs −$1.72) and wins 67% of its Lot-A trades. It still loses $1,127 more in total, because it takes 2,973 fills instead of 1,228. A tight exit re-arms the slot constantly; each re-arm is another 1R stop and another $1.50. Judge cells on total money, not on hit-rate or on $/trade.

ER 0.20 – 0.35303−3,314−3,453−2,629−2,792the whole loss, in one band, at every exit setting. This is the "rotation legs that look trendy but reverse" band the 07-30 note identified — confirmed on 5× the sample.

ER ≥ 0.50 (the BIG-TREND rung)6−370−336−367−197n=6 — no read at all, but nothing here says the RIDE rung is being missed
The awkward implication for the ER floor

The 07-30 diagnosis was right — the 0.20–0.35 band is the bleed. But the fix that was shipped is a floor at 0.35, and on this tape a floor at 0.35 would delete the only profitable population (ER < 0.20, +$2,233) while keeping a band that is red on 65 fills. A band-exclusion (skip 0.20–0.35) is what the data supports; a floor is not. Filtering the sim to ER ≥ 0.35 leaves 71 fills worth −$1,035; filtering to both designed floors (ER ≥ 0.35 and ATR ≥ 24) leaves 15 fills in 16 sessions, worth −$246 — the floors as written do not sharpen the gate, they silence it. Flagging, not actioning: that is an arming decision and it belongs to you and the router, not to exit_overrides.json.

The time-of-day axis and the regime axis disagree: regime says go wider, the US afternoon says go tighter. exit_overrides.json is a single static cell and cannot express either. That is an argument for the router eventually owning the exit axis the way it owns arming — not an argument for picking the tighter cell because one 255-fill window prefers it.

Fact table 9 — the thing I found on the way (and it is not small)

REV2·Q1 asked what the machine does with your hands off. Part of the answer is that grind_long's own entry filters are not running.

FactEvidence
The filters existdeciders.ER_FLOOR = {"grind_long": 0.35} (added 07-30) and ATR_FLOOR = {"grind_long": 24.0} (07-25 rehab)
They are applied by sub-slot tagtournament.py:139/142 call er_blocks(slot) / atr_blocks(slot) with the raw intent tag. Under the scale-out slate that tag is grind_long_A / grind_long_B. _base() is applied to the bench-list, the 55s veto and the exhaustion confirm — but not to these two.
Live proofOf the 40 grind_long_A/_B fills booked after the ER floor went in on 07-30: 31 had entry ER below 0.35 and 22 had entry ATR below 24; 33 of 40 violated at least one floor. Lowest ER filled: 0.03. Lowest ATR filled: 11.2.

Not actioned here — this section is read-only on live config. It belongs on the Saturday card as a code fix plus a threshold decision, taken together.

Fact table 10 — the tail question, answered directly

The standing objection to any capped exit is that grind's live profit lives in the tail: CHANDELIER + MANUAL_CLAIM exits, 16 trades, +$1,566. A 3.5R cap has to be judged against that, not against an average.

Where the tail money actually isn$share
Sim, WIDE lot — winning exits below 2R47+1,31210.4%
Sim, WIDE lot — winning exits 2R to 3.5R7+5614.5%
Sim, WIDE lot — winning exits above 3.5R52+10,67885.1% (81.4% above 5R alone)
Live, the 16 tail trades — exits at or under 3.5R13+1,03666%
Live, the 16 tail trades — exits above 3.5R3+53034%

Both things are true and they point the same way. In the sim the tail is enormous — 85% of the wide lot's gross profit comes from exits beyond 3.5R, so capping there gives up something real. But the wide lot as a whole still nets −$938, because it pays for that tail with give-back on every trade that peaks and reverses. And in the live book, two-thirds of the celebrated "tail" money came out at or under 3.5R anyway — a 3.5R target would have banked $1,036 of the $1,566 and given up $530. That is why the recommendation keeps Lot B on the wide chandelier: you keep an uncapped lot for the genuine runners and you take the certain money slightly later on Lot A. What the data forbids is capping both lots tight, which is precisely what M5 proposed.

Fact table 11 — reproducing (and refuting) the Part 2 · LAB cell

M5 came from the Friday lab: grind_long → {a_r 0.5, b 1.0}, off a MED-TREND cell of n=78 at +$10.3/trade and 59% win, with SCALP-CHOP n=542 agreeing. I re-ran it the lab's own way (paired, same-entry repricing) but with two corrections: 250 ms ticks instead of 5-second bars, and grind_long only (the lab's cell pooled both directions; only the long side is armed).

Rung (lab's own definition)nA0.5/B1.0 $/signalwin %A2.5+WIDEA3.0+WIDEWIDE×2verdict on the lab's claim
MED-TREND (ER 0.30–0.50)121−5.1946−20.58−18.85−29.29half-reproduced. The relative claim survives — tight does beat the 2.5/wide guess here. The absolute claim does not: +$10.3 at 59% win becomes −$5.19 at 46% win once you price on ticks and drop the short side. You cannot build a live cell on a number that is negative.
SCALP-CHOP (ER < 0.30)777−0.8452+7.57+9.57+11.01refuted outright. The lab said this bucket also picks 0.5/1.0. On ticks it picks the widest thing available, by $12 a signal on 777 signals.
BIG-TREND (ER ≥ 0.50)4−97.51—−108.68−108.68−108.68n=4 on the long side — no read, as REV2·Q3 warned

And on the sequential (policy) sim, which is the one that counts, the SCALP-CHOP rung is −$1,742 for 0.5/1.0, +$53 for the loaded default, +$1,242 for A3.5/B3.5. M5 should not be actioned. Two things caused the original result: 5-second repricing (which flatters tight exits, because a 5s bar cannot show you the tick that stopped you out first), and pooling grind_short into a cell used to set a long-only gate.

Fact table 12 — what each lever is actually worth (16 sessions, tick-repriced)

The point of the whole exercise: the exit knob is the small one.

LeverChangenet $deltarobustness
nothingloaded default A2.5 / wide−2,115—10 of 16 sessions green
EXIT→ A3.0 / wide (recommended)−1,498+617direction proven, size not (see fact table 5)
EXIT→ A3.5 / B3.5 (best stats, a spike)−414+1,702passes LODO, strip-3, bootstrap and both halves — fails plateau
EXIT→ A0.5 / B1.0 (M5)−3,242−1,127fails everything
ARMINGdefault exit, but never arm in "building"+1,027+3,14210/16 green, LODO worst −$116, archive half −$2,375 — fails the time split
ARMINGdefault exit, only arm when entry ER < 0.20+2,233+4,3489/16 green, LODO worst +$1,002, strip-top-3-days −$935, archive half −$1,150 — fails the time split
BOTHA3.0/wide + only arm at ER < 0.20+2,645+4,76012/16 green, LODO worst +$1,308 — same time-split failure

Read the last three rows with discipline. The arming levers are worth 3–5× the exit lever in dollars, and every one of them is green only in the capture half and red in the archive half. The exit direction is the opposite: A3.5/B3.5 beats the default by +$997 in the archive half and +$705 in the capture half. So the finding I am willing to sign is the small one, and the big one goes on the watch list with a date on it.

The verdict, in the order you should read it

1. Change the cell, but change it small. Write {"grind_long": {"a_r": 3.0, "b": "wide"}} — Lot A 2.5R→3.0R, Lot B untouched. Worth +$617 over 16 sessions against the loaded default's −$2,115. This is also the exact cell REV2·Q3 pre-registered for Monday 2026-08-03 and pinned until the 08-21 grade date, so writing it costs nothing in test integrity and gains the pin. It is a move along a proven direction, not a proven number.

2. Do not action M5. {a_r 0.5, b 1.0} is −$1,127 against the default, green on 5 sessions of 16, and fails leave-one-day-out, strip-the-best-3 and the bootstrap. The number that motivated it (+$10.3/tr, 59% win, n=78) does not survive being priced on ticks and restricted to the long side; it becomes −$5.19/signal at 46% win. Delete the item.

3. The RIDE rung is not the question it was made out to be. On 16 tick-covered sessions the BIG-TREND cell for grind_long is n=6, and the wide lock-chandelier is mid-pack everywhere: −$938 for the lot, no better than a plain 3.0R target. Keep it on Lot B because an uncapped lot is cheap insurance against the runner, not because it has been shown to earn.

4. The real money is in arming, and it is not proven yet. Keeping grind_long out of the ER 0.20–0.35 / "building" tape is worth +$3,100 to +$4,800 on the same 16 sessions — 5× the exit lever — but it is green in the capture half and red in the archive half, so it is a direction with a strong prior, not a measurement. It needs the 5-second back-extension (REV2·Q3 Track A) to be gradeable, and it belongs to the router, not to the exit file.

5. And the honest floor: grind_long is red at every exit setting on this tape. The best of 130 configurations still loses $414 over 16 sessions, before a penny of slippage. No exit cell rescues this gate. Choosing the cell is damage control while the arming question gets answered.

Method & limits — read before quoting any number above

  • Every sweep number is a ceiling. Fills are at the exact stop/target/trail level with no slippage and $1.50 round-turn fees. Measured against the 40 real STOP fills in this gate's own live history, real stops filled 7% worse than the model; the desk's standing finding is that live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled. Every dollar figure here is the best case, and the best case is negative.
  • Exits are priced on 250 ms ticks only. 15.3M archive ticks (07-05→07-17) plus 10.1M capture ticks (07-23→07-31). No exit anywhere in this section was decided on a bar. The 15 further sessions that have 5-second bars but no ticks were excluded from every number.
  • Sample. 5,874 grind_long signal-minutes on the 31-session tape; 3,064 of them on the 16 tick-covered sessions; 1,228 lot-fills for the baseline cell after the one-position-at-a-time slot logic. Regime buckets: whipsaw 452, normal-chop 393, building 266, clean up-trend 106, dead-chop 11. Anything under n ≈ 150 in this section is flagged inline and is a direction, not a measurement — that covers clean up-trend, US-open, ER ≥ 0.35 and the whole BIG-TREND rung.
  • Density. The sim takes ~77 lot-fills a session; the live gate, with router benching, took ~20. Rank configs on these numbers; discount the dollars ~3.8× before treating them as a forecast.
  • Marked-to-market residue. 14% of entries neither stop, target nor trail inside the 120-minute MAX_HOLD and are marked at the last tick — the same entries in every config, so they do not tilt one config against another.
  • Not tested here. The give-back overlay (off for both lots under this slate), the adaptive_exit selector (also off under scale-out), and the 5-second back-extension to 06-22. All three are separate questions.
  • Read-only. No live file was modified — gate_switches.env, exit_overrides.json and every service are untouched. Writing the recommended cell requires an edit plus a tournament restart, and it is reversible by deleting the key.

REV3 Consistency audit — did the Rev2 fold-in actually land in the published report?

Garrath — short answer: no, and not by a little. Five agents wrote five Rev2 sections. The fold-in put two paragraphs and one table into the published report and stopped. Q0 (the Monday action card), Q3 (the n=5→n=24 correction) and Q4 (the router's real value) are not in the published report in any form — zero words, zero numbers. Q1 contributed its opening callout and nothing else — not its exit sweep, not its Thursday reconstruction, not its re-nomination. Q2 contributed one correction paragraph — not its exit-cell sweep, and critically not the dead-floors finding. The good news buried inside the bad: the specific hazard you were worried about — Q0's superseded rows M4 and M6 sitting in the report telling a reader to do the wrong thing — did not happen, because Q0 never made it in either. The hazard that did happen is the mirror image: the report still says everything Q1–Q4 were written to overturn, and none of the overturning.

★ The single worst line in the published report, and it was introduced BY the fold-in

The Part 2 "Correction (applied 08-01)" paragraph — one of only two Rev2 patches that landed — states: "live abs_veto_long made +$326 over 47 trades this week". That number is wrong. Straight from data/gazbot7.db, abs_veto_long over the report's own week is +$59.5 over 59 lots (43 signals). No window produces $326/47 — Mon–Fri gives +$131.0/56, from-07-28 gives +$177.0/52. It also contradicts the same document's Part 1 card table, which correctly prints abs_veto_long | +59.5 | 59. A correction paragraph carrying a $266.5 error, in the section whose whole job is honesty, is the most damaging single defect in the artefact.

The contradictions still LIVE in the published report — ranked by money

1 · The router's own value: −$1,216 is printed twice, and it is wrong by $1,466

Where the superseded claim still isVerbatim
Part 2.5 §3 "Router week-review", WEEK rowWeek | — | $15,788 | $17,003 | −$1,216 | −$1,083
Part 2.6 §3, WEEK rowWEEK | — | $15,788 | $17,003 | −$1,216 | $0 (durable days)
Part 2.6 §3, prose"the raw net is −$1,216, but two things inflate the 'missed' column — the dual A/B slots double-count one signal, and the giant 'missed' figures on 07-29 … are dominated by grind_long shadow winning during brief benched windows"
Part 2.6 §8 verdict"Is the router earning its keep? Yes — as a defense."
Where the correction appearsNowhere. Q4 is absent from the published report.

Would a reader be misled, and by how much? Yes, in three separate ways. (i) He reads a $1,216 deficit that is 60% bookkeeping — Q4 proves the A/B legs are byte-identical in the nightly's own JSON (grind_long_A and grind_long_B both 116 / 46 won / $2,633.5 / 70 lost / −$2,228.5) because reprice_scalp() ignores the slot suffix. Merging them takes the week from −$1,101 to −$440, recovering $661. (ii) The report names the double-count and then leaves it in the total anyway — that is worse than not noticing, because it signals the author knew. (iii) He is handed a "Yes" verdict off a statistic that flips sign on leave-one-day-out (−$804 dropping 07-30, +$230 dropping 07-29) and therefore supports no verdict at all.

The money he doesn't get told about: +$1,615/week. Q4's segment split is the biggest number in the entire Rev2 round and it is robust in a way almost nothing else in this report is — CHOP benching +$1,088 at zero haircut, TREND_UP benching −$1,380 needing a 52% winner haircut to break even, and with the TREND_UP benches removed the week is positive in all nine robustness folds (+$533 to +$1,021). That is the highest-value, best-evidenced change in the report and the published report does not contain it.

Exact minimal fix

In Part 2.6 §3, replace the sentence beginning "Honest reading (this number is noisier than it looks): the raw net is −$1,216…" with: "Corrected (08-01): −$1,216 counts every suppressed signal twice — the dual-slot A and B legs are byte-identical in the nightly's own output because the repricer ignores the slot suffix. Merged, the week is 353 signals, $8,644 saved, $9,084 missed, net −$440. And the blend hides two opposite, individually-robust halves: benching in CHOP is +$1,088 at zero haircut; benching in TREND_UP is −$1,380 and would need the shadow to overstate winners by 52% to break even. Stop benching momentum in a confirmed TREND_UP and the same week is +$1,175 — a +$1,615 swing, positive in all nine leave-one-day-out and strip-the-best folds." Change both WEEK-row net cells from −$1,216 to −$440 (A/B merged; −$1,216 as originally double-counted). In §8, change "Is the router earning its keep? Yes — as a defense." to "Split verdict. The defence earns its keep decisively (+$1,088 in CHOP, +$613 on fader benches, robust to every fold). The offence does not (−$1,380 benching momentum in TREND_UP). The blended number has no statistical standing — it flips sign on which single day you drop." Add a row 8 to the Saturday table: "Stop benching momentum in a confirmed TREND_UP | +$1,615/wk at zero haircut, positive in 9/9 folds | RULE — already banked as [[us-open-dont-bench-trend-rider]], simply not enforced."

2 · Every ER and ATR floor on the desk has been dead since 07-29 16:24 — and the report reasons from them five times

This is Q2 §3 and it is the most consequential factual correction of the whole Rev2 round. tournament.step() passes the sub-slot tag (grind_long_A) to er_blocks/atr_blocks, but ER_FLOOR/ATR_FLOOR are keyed on base names, so the lookup never matches. The journal shows 7,734 suppression lines up to 2026-07-29 13:29:04 and zero since. Five gates affected: grind_long (ER 0.35 + ATR 24), abs_veto_long (ER 0.25), abs_veto_short (ATR 16), capitulation_long (ATR 10). None of this appears in the published report.

Where the report reasons from a floor that is not runningVerbatim
Part 1, gate × day grid"That is not a flaw — it's what the 0.35 ER floor is for, keeping it dormant unless the day really trends"
Part 1.5 §2 grind_long"Two live floors are actively destroying the gate. The ER ≥ 0.35 floor is backwards … The ATR ≥ 24 floor is far too tight for this regime."
Part 2.5 §2 "The GRIND lead""Grind fired 52 of 56 times below 0.35 on this measure … Whatever ER the live gate is computing, it is not the trend-efficiency the floor claims to guard … ER filters are fake here"
Part 2.6 §2, CHURN row"grind's 0.35 ER floor + abs_veto's veto meant most premature arms never fired — process error, not $ error"
Part 2.6 §4 + §8 action 6"The grind 0.35 ER ARM floor … is validated separately (+$728 over 10d) — leave it" · "arm once on ER≥0.35 sustained … grind self-gates via its 0.35 floor"
Where the correction appearsNowhere.

Would a reader be misled? Badly, and in the most expensive direction. Part 2.5 §2 gropes at the truth and lands on the wrong explanation: it observes grind firing 52 of 56 times below its floor and concludes ER filters are fake on this tape — a claim about the market. The real cause is a lookup bug — a claim about our code. Those two conclusions point at completely different fixes. Meanwhile Part 2.6 §8 tells Garrath his churner "self-gates via its 0.35 floor", i.e. tells him to rely on a safety feature that has not executed once since Wednesday. And Part 2.6 §2 grades a router CHURN as costing $0 on the strength of that same absent floor.

Exact minimal fix

Insert a red callout immediately under the Part 2.5 §2 heading and repeat it under Part 2.6 §4: "★ Correction (08-01): ER_FLOOR and ATR_FLOOR have not executed since 2026-07-29 16:24. tournament.step() passes the sub-slot tag to er_blocks/atr_blocks, which are keyed on base names, so the lookup never matches — 7,734 suppressions before 07-29 13:29, zero after; five gates affected. Everything below that reasons from a live floor is reasoning about a filter that is not running. Grind did not fire 52 of 56 times below its floor because ER filters are fake; it fired because there was no floor. Fix the wiring first, then re-derive each floor on tape that was actually filtered — do not let a silent bug decide the floor's value." Then delete the clause "grind self-gates via its 0.35 floor" from Part 2.6 §8 action 6, and change Part 1.5 §2's "Two live floors are actively destroying the gate" to "Two floors are configured against this gate — and neither has executed since 07-29 (see the correction in Part 2.5); as written they are backwards and too tight, which is why they must not be re-wired at their current values."

3 · exhaustion_short is still crowned "the survivor" in five places; the correction sits in exactly one box

The fold-in did put the honest number at the very top of Part 1 — credit where due, a reader starting at page one meets −$56 over 44 automated fires before anything else. But nothing downstream was touched, so the two readings sit ninety words apart and then diverge for the rest of the document.

aPart 1, "Read this first — what SURVIVED the week" (immediately below the correction callout)"exhaustion_short was green on 4 of the 5 sessions … 54% of its trades won, +$316 for the week. It is the steadiest earner on the desk."
bPart 1, six-gate card table, verdict cellexhaustion_short | +316.5 | 48 | 54 | … | SURVIVOR — steadiest

dPart 1, Case 1 Thursday"exhaustion_short banked four fade-claims in a row for +$372.5 … judge this gate on its live fades, not its fixed-target shadow"
ePart 2, Relegation view, exhaustion row"LIVE exhaustion_short is the desk's cleanest fader … Judge it on live fills."
fPart 2.6 §2, graded-ledger table"07-30 14:57 arm short-side fade book … ✅ GOOD | +$372 | exhaustion_short went 4/4 live fading the roll-over — best offense of the day"
—CorrectionPart 1 callout only: "−$56.0 | 44 | +372.5 | 4 | +316.5" and "unattended it is roughly flat-to-negative"

Would a reader be misled, and by how much? A reader going strictly top-to-bottom gets the correction first — so the acute risk is low. But it does not survive contact with the rest of the document: rows (b) and (c) re-assert the crown within two screens, and rows (e) and (f) are in sections a reader will very reasonably jump straight to. Row (f) is the sharpest: the router is graded ✅ GOOD and credited +$372 for a sequence that was four hand-clicks. Q1 repriced those exact four legs on 250 ms tape under the exit config actually loaded at the time and got −$51.2. So the router's best-graded offensive call of the week is worth −$51, not +$372 — a $423 swing inside the router's own scorecard, which is the section that then concludes the router earns its keep.

Exact minimal fix — six cells, no re-analysis needed

(a) "+$316 for the week. It is the steadiest earner on the desk." → "+$316 for the week — but $372.5 of that was four trades you closed by hand. Automated it is −$56 over 44 fires (see the callout above). It is the steadiest thing on the desk, not the biggest earner: 50% win, a median trade of essentially zero, and the smallest leave-one-day-out damage of any gate that traded in size."   (b) verdict cell "SURVIVOR — steadiest" → "FLAT AUTOMATED (−$56 / 44) — the +$316 includes 4 hand-claims".   (c) "That row is why exhaustion is the survivor." → "That row is why exhaustion is the steadiest — but read it against the automated split above: unattended the row is flat."   (d) append: "All four of those were MANUAL_CLAIM exits. Repriced on 250 ms tape under the exit config actually loaded, the same four entries would have been −$51.2 — three of them were sitting on positions the tape later ran 49 to 98 points through the stop."   (e) "the desk's cleanest fader" → "the desk's steadiest fader — though its live green is hand-claim dependent (−$56 over 44 automated fires)".   (f) $ cell "+$372" → "+$372 hand-claimed / −$51 automated" and grade "✅ GOOD" → "✅ GOOD (regime read) / ⚠ the $ credit is manual".

4 · BIG-TREND is stated as n=5 and the report refuses an archive it had already used

Where the correction appearsNowhere. Q3 is absent.

Would a reader be misled? Yes, three ways, and one of them is a factual falsehood in the report's honesty showcase. (i) The cell is n=24, not 5 — five is the count of live grind_fast shadow entries in an ER≥0.50 minute; the ladder verdict was computed on a 24-entry tick-tape replay. The caveat quotes the live number in a caveat about the replay number. (ii) The refusal of the archive is false, and the lab had already used it — 11 of the 24 BIG-TREND entries come from that pre-capture stretch, and the "different contract era" objection dies on the seam: last archive tick 29,623.00 at 15:35:29.973, first capture bar opens 29,622.75 at 15:35:30, r=0.9998 across 2,925 overlapping minutes. Same instrument, same front month. A report that invents a principled refusal for tape it silently consumed is worse off than one that never mentioned it. (iii) The rung is not a hypothetical — it is live right now. I checked data/exit_overrides.json: it keys only exhaustion_short, abs_veto_long and abs_veto_short. grind_long has no entry, so it inherits the built-in _BIG_RUN default, which is literally Lot A @ 2.5R + Lot B wide lock-chandelier. The report frames it as a stone left unturned; it is an unhedged live position.

And the bigger prize is missing too. Q3's unasked-for finding: on grind's MED-TREND rung, n=326, the live A2.5+WIDE default makes +$8.49/trade against TIGHTx2's +$17.60 — a $9.11 × 326 gap with real n behind it, needing no new tape at all. That is a better-evidenced number than the BIG-TREND question the caveat spends four hundred words on, and it is nowhere in the published report.

Exact minimal fix

Table cell "BIG-TREND (5)" → "BIG-TREND (24)"; verdict cell "UNTESTED" → "DIRECTION SUPPORTED (bootstrap P=0.95, plateau 2.5–3.5R, both time-halves agree) · MAGNITUDE UNPROVEN (strip-3 takes +$62 to +$10)". In the ★ CRITICAL caveat, delete from "I did not manufacture a BIG-TREND sample…" through "…does not contain a clean trend week for grind" and replace with: "Eleven of the 24 BIG-TREND entries already come from the pre-capture MNQ archive (ticks.db, 07-05→07-15) — an earlier draft refused that archive as 'a different contract era', which was wrong on the facts and wrong about our own lab, which had already used it. The tapes join one tick apart (29,623.00 → 29,622.75, r=0.9998 over 2,925 overlapping minutes): same instrument, same front month. What is genuinely missing is n, not honesty — the pre-registered grade needs 39 paired entries (derived from a paired sd of $247 on a $79.4 diff) and we have 24. And note this rung is LIVE today: grind_long has no key in exit_overrides.json, so it inherits the built-in A@2.5R + wide lock-chandelier. This is not an unturned stone, it is an unhedged position." In the policy table change "wide-chandelier (unproven, n=5)" → "wide-chandelier — LIVE TODAY by default, n=24, under pre-registered test to 2026-08-21". Add one row to the same table: "grind_long | MED-TREND | n=326: live wide default +$8.49/tr vs tight ×2 +$17.60/tr — the biggest evidenced exit gap on the desk, and it needs no new tape."

Where the correction appearsNowhere. Q2 §6 is absent.

Would a reader be misled, and by how much? Yes — this is the one where the report actively instructs inaction on a change two independent sweeps say to make. Q2 §6 replayed 81 abs_veto_55s LONG signals on 250 ms ticks (validated against the shadow repricer at 98.7% sign agreement, MAE $2.70) across eleven exit cells. The live A1.5/B2.5 ranks last on every measure: +$13.6/signal vs +$22.7 for A1.0/B1.5, LODO-min +$4.7 vs +$16.8, strip-best-3 +$6.8 vs +$19.0 — and the winner sits on a broad plateau (0.75/1.5, 1.0/1.5, 1.0/2.0 all within $4), not a spike. Q1 reached the same conclusion by a different route on 42 automated entries: plateau at 0.75–1.0R (+$292 / +$532) collapsing to +$26 at exactly the loaded 1.5R. Two independent sweeps, two different samples, same answer. Worth roughly +$9.1 per signal × 81 signals ≈ +$737 on the swept book, ~+$390/week at the live rate of 43 long signals. The short side genuinely is noise (all cells +$15–18) and should be left at 1.5/2.5, exactly as the report says — but the report does not distinguish the sides.

Note the collision the report creates on its own. The published sentence "the exit is not the lever" is derived from the raw un-vetoed thrust rows, then applied by name to abs_veto, which is a different, filtered population. That is a sample switch inside one sentence, and it is what produced the wrong instruction.

Exact minimal fix

Change the policy-table cell for thrust / abs_veto to: "the raw un-vetoed thrust cannot be rescued by an exit — but the LIVE abs_veto_long cell can, and it is the worst cell on the board. A tick-honest sweep of 81 long signals ranks the loaded A1.5/B2.5 last of eleven (+$13.6/sig, LODO-min +$4.7) against A1.0/B1.5 (+$22.7, LODO-min +$16.8) on a broad plateau. Change exit_overrides.json → abs_veto_long: {a_r 1.0, b 1.5}. Leave abs_veto_short at 1.5/2.5 — the R choice is genuinely noise on that side (+$15 to +$18 at every cell). No exit rescues abs_veto_short in violent whipsaw (−$20 to −$33 at every R from 0.5 to 6.0); only not trading it does." Delete the sentence "abs_veto's dollars come from its entry veto, not its exit R's" or qualify it to "most of abs_veto's edge is the entry veto — but the long side's R's are still the worst cell swept." Rewrite the bullet "for these three, the exit is not the lever" to name the population: "for the raw, un-vetoed versions of these three the exit is not the lever."

Would a reader be misled? He is given three recommendations in one section that do not agree with each other, and then the actual sweep answer is missing. There is a second, structural error underneath it. The report treats exhaustion as one gate on one exit all week. It was not: until Wed 07-29 19:10 UTC it ran a single slot on the regime-3 adaptive chandelier (+$365 over 37 automated fills, PF 1.46, green 3 of 3 days); from that moment it ran two scale-out sub-slots at A@2.5R + wide lock-chandelier (−$421 over 7 fills at 14% win). I verified both halves against the ledger. That mislabel is what hides the causal story.

Exact minimal fix

In Part 2 · LAB (a), replace "the prior era (07-27–07-31, A@2.5R + chandelier, 37 trades) netted +$365 with a healthy 11 targets / 10 chandeliers / 16 stops mix" with: "the prior era was not the same machine. Until Wed 07-29 19:10 UTC exhaustion ran a SINGLE slot on the regime-3 adaptive chandelier: 37 automated fills, +$365, PF 1.46, green on all 3 of its days. The scale-out slate that replaced it (A@2.5R + wide lock-chandelier) is −$421 over 7 fills at 14% win. The gate did not break; its exit was swapped for a trend-riding one, and a fader does not get 2.5R in a whipsaw." Then add, in place of the bare "too thin to judge": "A 64-entry 250 ms sweep does grade the Rs even though the live trial cannot: the loaded 0.5R Lot A is the worst scalp rung on the ladder (+$306); the plateau is 0.75–1.0R (+$510 / +$450); and Lot B's steadiest config by a distance is a k1.5 chandelier (+$502, LODO floor +$330, worst day −$25). But the sweep's real message is that no exit is robustly green in violent whipsaw, where this gate took 28 of its 62 entries — keeping it benched there is worth more than any R."

The verdict

The report needs a Rev3 re-render before Garrath reads it. Not because it is dishonest in intent — the Part 1 callout is a genuinely brave opener and the thin-n discipline holds up all the way through Movement 3 — but because the fold-in landed 2 of 5 sections and left the document arguing with itself in seven places, telling him to leave two exit cells alone that two independent sweeps say to change, reasoning five times from filters that have not executed since Wednesday, quoting an n of 5 for a cell that is n=24, publishing a principled refusal of an archive the lab had already consumed, and omitting the single best-evidenced number of the week (+$1,615/wk from one router rule). One of the two patches that did land carries a $266.5 error that contradicts a table in the same document.

The good news is the fix is small. Every correction above already exists, computed and robustness-tested, in rev2_q1..q4.html. Nothing needs re-analysis. The work is: 3 sentence edits to propagate −$598; 6 cell/sentence edits for exhaustion; 2 for BIG-TREND; 2 for the abs_veto exit; 1 wrong number and 1 verdict pill in Part 2; 2 table rows and 1 paragraph for the router value; 1 correction callout for the dead floors; and 6 footnotes. Roughly two dozen targeted edits to the three section files, then re-render HTML and PDF together. Alternatively — and this is cleaner given the volume — append rev2_q1 through rev2_q4 as a Part 3 "Rev2 corrections" and put a pointer at the top of each superseded section. That preserves the audit trail, which is worth something in a report whose selling point is showing its own graves.

Audit scope: src/gazbot7/web_static/weekly_2026-07-31.html and .pdf read end to end; diffed against reports/friday_v7/sections/{part1_live,part2_shadow,part2_6}.bak; cross-read against rev2_q0..q4.html and docs/FRIDAY_V7_REPORT_SCOPE.md. All ledger figures re-verified against data/gazbot7.db, data/gate_switches.env and data/exit_overrides.json — nothing recalled. Read-only: no code changed, no service touched. reports/friday_v7/plays.json and src/gazbot7/web_static/monday_2026-07-31.html were out of scope and were neither read for reconciliation nor edited.

M1 The biggest runs on MNQ this week — did we show up?

Forget what the desk did this week — start from the raw tape. MNQ printed 73 runs of 1.5×ATR or bigger in the last seven days (a 15-minute move of at least 90 points, against a typical 15-min range of 60). That is the whole tape, not a top-ten. We caught 14, we were positioned against 6, and we sat out 53. The runs we aligned with banked +$944 of honest money — but that is only 20% of their $4689 hindsight ceiling, and the 53 we sat out left $14519 on the table. The bleed is not the runs; it is the fading we do around them.

THE FULL CENSUS

Every qualifying run, in order. Move is the swing in points; $ 1lot is that move on one MNQ lot ($2/pt) — the hindsight ceiling. real $ is what a gate actually banked near it. Flow is net aggressor volume in the 60s before; amp is pre-run amplitude; book is the far-side L2 depth share (below 0.50 = the side price ran toward was thin). Green = we caught it, red = we fought it.

The runs made money and the fading gave it back; we convert a fraction of the ceiling and ignore the rest. The next two movements ask whether we can do better — first with the gates we own, then with new ones.

M2 Could our own gates have caught the runs we sat out?

Garrath, this one nearly fooled me — so let me walk you through it honestly, because the honesty is the finding. I took the 53 runs we sat out this week (the frozen census — $14,519 of hindsight money left on the table) and fired all six of our live gates at them mechanically, ungated (no ER floor, no ATR floor, no absorption-confirm — the most generous version), in each run's own direction, anywhere in the 10 minutes before it lit up. Then I priced every exit honestly on this week's own trade ticks. The headline looked good: 21 fires, +$582, and it survives stripping the best three trades — nothing like last week's clean fluke. One gate, rgv_short, went four-for-four for +$478. But it is a mirage, and I can prove it in one number: fed only the 53 runs we already know happened, rgv_short makes +$478; fed the whole week's tape, the exact same gate makes +$1.80 a trade. The lab was quietly handing each fader only its winners. This is an honest null — just a subtler, better-disguised one than last week's.

What survived the skeptic — the one number that ends the argument

Nothing survived as a tradeable edge, and I will not dress the +$582 up as one. The durable fact is a base-rate: our turnback fader rgv_short throws 151 signals in a week, and only 6 of them (4%) are followed by a real run in its direction — while 13% go straight against it. The idle-gate lab only ever showed it the ~4 signals that preceded a sat-out down-run, so of course they printed 2R. That is survivorship, not skill: cherry-pick any early-entry fade's winners after the fact and it looks brilliant. Feed it the whole tape and it is a coin-flip that bleeds on costs. On top of that, the four biggest sat-out runs this week (+298, −276, −251, −248 — $2,146 of ceiling between them) drew ZERO fire from any gate. The money on the table belongs to triggers we do not own, which is Movement 3's job. Arming the gates we have is still a dead end — here is the proof, in full.

The scoreboard — all six gates, fired ungated at the 53 sat-out runs

In-direction only: the three long gates can only catch the 32 up-runs, the three short gates only the 21 down-runs — so "eligible" is how many sat-out runs each gate could physically have caught. One position per fire, first trigger in the 10-minute pre-run window wins, exits repriced tick-by-tick on this week's tape (grind and thrust ride the chandelier plus the native 1-ATR stop; the faders take a 2R scalp or the 1-ATR stop). Honest money, the same week the runs happened. Green = it made money over its handful of fires, red = it lost.

All of the "profit" is in the two turnback faders (rgv_short +$478, rgv_long +$278 = +$756 between them). The two momentum gates net −$174 (thrust_short stopped out all four times), and the two footprint gates never fired at all. On its face rgv_short looks like the best thing in this whole report. It is the most dangerous line in it — and the next table is why.

The survivorship killer — fed the winners vs fed the whole week

This is the test that matters. The lab only shows a gate the 53 ignitions we already know turned into runs — that is picking the answers before the exam. So I ran each gate a second way: fired it ungated across the entire week's tape, every time it triggered, repriced on the same ticks. The gap between the two columns is the size of the lie.

GateAt the known runs
(fires · net · $/fire · win)		Fed the WHOLE week
(trades · net · $/trade · win)		Verdict
rgv_short4 · +$478 · +$119.5 · 100%134 · +$243 · +$1.8 · 34%SURVIVORSHIP
rgv_long6 · +$278 · +$46.3 · 67%120 · +$45 · +$0.4 · 36%SURVIVORSHIP
thrust_short4 · −$225.5 · −$56.4 · 0%113 · −$552 · −$4.9 · 35%REAL LOSER
grind_long7 · +$52 · +$7.4 · 43%239 · +$747 · +$3.1 · 38%RIDES TRENDS, NOT IGNITIONS

Read the rgv_short row slowly: the +$478 that made it look like a star is 4 cherry-picked winners out of 134 fires — the other 130 fires that week netted about −$235, and the gate's true worth is +$1.80 a trade, a 34% win-rate, a hair above breakeven. rgv_long is the same story at $0.40 a trade. thrust_short is honestly consistent: it loses at the runs (−$225) and loses all week (−$552). grind_long is the one green full-week number, but look at where it earns — only +$52 at the ignitions themselves (a coin-flip), because grind does not catch a run starting, it rides a trend already running; its +$747 is a trend-week story that belongs in the rehab section, not here.

The base-rate — how rare a "run" is under a fader's signal

Here is the survivorship point stated as plainly as I can. For each gate I counted every ungated signal it fired all week, then asked how many were actually followed by a 90-point-plus move in its own direction within 15 minutes — a real run — versus how many went straight against it.

GateSignals / weekFollowed by a real run (≥90pt)≥150pt runWent ≥40pt adverse
rgv_short1516 (4%)219 (13%)
rgv_long1309 (7%)125 (19%)
thrust_short1186 (5%)325 (21%)

Four to seven percent. That is how often one of our triggers is standing in front of a real run when it fires — and it goes hard against the gate two to five times more often than it precedes a run. The lab handed each fader only that thin 4–7% slice and called it a gate. It is not. It is a gate that fires a hundred-and-fifty times to be right six.

The other two skeptic cuts — strip-the-best and the R-target sweep

Even before the survivorship test, two standard robustness checks flag the +$582 as fragile. First, strip out the biggest winners: the edge halves at three and flips negative at five. Second — the operator's rule that no R-target is a truth — I re-priced all ten fader fires at every scalp target from 0.5R to 3.0R. A real edge shows a plateau (a best R with a roll-off either side). This just climbs forever.

Strip test (all 21 fires)TradesNet $R-sweep (10 fader fires)Net $Win%
Keep all21+$582.5 0.5R+$17590%
Strip best 318+$183.0 1.0R+$41490%
Strip best 516−$43.0 1.5R+$54080%
    2.0R+$75680%
    2.5R+$97380%
    3.0R+$1,18980%

The R-sweep never rolls over — wider is always better, right out to 3R. That is not "2R is the wrong target," it is the survivorship effect showing through the numbers: because every fire we measured was in front of a run that kept going, any bigger target banks more. It is the runs being real, not the fade being good. With n=10 and no plateau, there is no R to trust here.

The control that proves it is a seeing problem, not an exit problem

To be fair to the exits, I ran a blind control: ignore every gate, just enter in the run's direction at the moment of ignition and ride the chandelier. That is cheating — it uses hindsight to know the direction — but it isolates the question "if we simply showed up pointed the right way, does the exit keep the money?"

Blind in-direction entry at ignition (all 53 runs)RunsNet $$/runWin%
Keep all53+$4,076+$76.975%
Strip best 350+$3,405+$68.1—
Strip best 548+$2,998+$62.5—

Show up pointed the right way and the runs pay +$4,076 at 75% win, and it barely dents when you strip the best five — because the money is real and directional, spread across dozens of runs, not two flukes. So the exits are fine and the money is genuinely there. The entire problem is knowing, in real time and without hindsight, which way and when — and that is exactly what our gates fail at: they only see 20 of the 53, and the versions that do fire early (the faders) fire everywhere and bleed. The exit is not the bottleneck. Vision is.

Where the money actually was — the 12 biggest runs we sat out

The $14,519 is concentrated in the monsters, so the only question that counts is whether arming our gates caught the big ones. Here are the twelve largest sat-out runs (worth $4,700 of ceiling) and exactly what our gates did on each.

Not a fought trade, not a stopped trade — nothing. When the move is genuinely huge, our triggers are asleep. The gates catch the mid-sized runs when a fade happens to line up, and no-show the feasts. That is the null in one sentence.

The segment split — is there a regime where arming pays?

The operator's rule is never to blanket one number across all the tape, so I split the 21 fires by time-of-day and by cause-cluster. If there is a corner where arming these gates genuinely works, it should light up here. It does not — the fires are thin and scattered in every bucket, and the "green" is the same survivorship winners re-sorted.

SegmentRunsFiresNet $Win%Read

No segment rescues it. The best-looking bucket, OPEN/NEWS at 75%, is four fires on eleven runs — the other seven news-runs drew nothing. VACUUM (snapback / stop-run) draws the most fires and the worst win-rate, exactly as you'd expect: our faders reach for the retrace and the vacuum keeps going. There is no regime here that says "arm these on this kind of day." The pattern in every bucket is the same — a few survivorship winners and a lot of no-shows.

Why each gate misses — the mechanism, gate by gate

None of this is bad luck; each miss is baked into what the gate is built to look for. This is the useful part — it tells us the trigger we are missing.

GateFiresWhy it can't reliably catch these runs
rgv_short / rgv_long4 / 6The turnback faders need price already stretched 2+ ATR past VWAP and rolling back. When a run happens to launch off exactly that setup, the fader gets in early at the top/bottom and rides 2R — which is why they "win" here. But that setup fires 130–150 times a week and only 4–7% of those precede a run; the rest chop or reverse. You cannot harvest the winners without taking all 150 fires, and the full basket is breakeven. This is the survivorship trap in one line.
thrust_short4 / 21Wants a five-bar (five-minute) momentum burst with a volume surge. These ignitions fire in one or two bars — by the time five bars confirm, the move is spent, so it arms late and buys the exhaustion. All four fires stopped out (−$225), and it loses all week too (−$552). A structurally late trigger on fast moves.
grind_long7 / 32Needs an established up-trend — VWAP sloping up with price riding above it. It arms once a trend is already running, so at the ignition itself it is a coin-flip (+$52, 43%). It earns its full-week +$747 by riding trends that are already underway, not by catching a run start — a different job from this section's.
capitulation_long0 / 32Fades a one-sided aggressor climax — one side running 3× its baseline AND 70%+ of flow, price already flushing that way. The up-runs we sat out do not start from a sell-flush blow-off; they ignite from quiet or flow-led setups. The trigger never trips. (Also L2-blind before 07-27 21:33 — a data gap on top of the logic gap.)
exhaustion_short0 / 21Needs heavy buying absorbed into a resting ask wall without price moving. Our down-runs are flow-led drives where the aggression does move price — so the "absorbed, price stuck" precondition fails and it never fires. Same early-week L2 blindness caveat applies.
Verdict

HONEST NULL DON'T ARM Firing all six live gates at the money we left on the table produces a headline +$582 that dissolves the moment you stop cheating: the two faders that carry it are worth +$1.80 and +$0.40 a trade fed the real tape, their signals precede a run only 4–7% of the time, the strip-5 test flips the whole thing negative, the R-sweep has no plateau, and the four biggest runs of the week drew zero fire. The blind control proves the money and the exits are both fine — the gap is purely that we cannot see these runs coming in real time. Do not arm anything off this section. The sat-out money belongs to triggers we don't own yet, and hunting those is Movement 3.

Be honest about the caveats: this is one week, in-sample, with tiny counts at the runs (21 fires; the faders just 10). The two footprint gates were L2-blind on the 07-27 daytime runs, so their zero is partly a data gap, not only a logic one. And I'll flag the one genuinely interesting thread, because it would be dishonest to bury it: the turnback-fade mechanism is the only thing we own that shows up early on these runs at all — if we ever find a way to fire it only in that 4–7% slice (a regime read that separates "stretched-and-turning into a real run" from "stretched-and-turning into more chop"), it becomes the entry we're missing. That separation is the stone left unturned, and it is a Movement-3 hunt, not a knob on the gate we have.

M3 The greenfield lab — inventing brand-new entries for the runs we sat out, and a chop-day scalp lab

Garrath — this is the flagship, and unlike last week it does not end in a clean sweep of graves. Something survived. I threw away every gate the desk owns, went back to a blank sheet, and asked one question nine different ways: can a brand-new mechanical entry show up for the 53 runs we sat out this week (— $14,519 of hindsight money on the table)? One candidate came through the whole robustness battery alive: NIPC, a news-window impulse-pullback entry, +$2,676 over 12 days, 171 trades, 43.3% win, $15.6 a trade, 44 of 44 parameter variants positive, both sides independently green, and it survives $12/round-trip and a 3-point stop-slip stress. Verdict: SHADOW. Never live, not yet — one regime-fortnight is not a promotion. Everything else in the greenfield lane is buried, including the entire flow-footprint family. And I ran the operator's chop-day scalp lab on top — the honest answer there is a NULL, but it comes with the most valuable single number in the section: on MNQ the whole visible 10-deep order book is 86 contracts against 2,664 contracts a minute of flow, so "absorption" is not a thing you can measure on this instrument. That fact explains four separate graves at once.

Lead with what SURVIVED — three things, in money order

1. `news_impulse_pullback` (NIPC) — a real, robust, brand-new entry. +$2,676 · n=171 · 12 days · 43.3% win · $15.6/trade · 9 of 12 days green · worst day −$176. It waits for a 2-minute impulse in the 13:00–15:00 UTC window, then refuses to chase it — it demands a 35–75% retrace and enters only on the half-back reclaim. It clears every kill-test that buried last week's candidates. SHADOW-first, explicitly not live.

2. The size-threshold answer — the question this whole exercise exists to answer. For tape flow there is no size at which a footprint becomes tradeable, and the relationship is inverted: across the 53 sat-out runs, corr(|move|, aligned pre-run flow) = −0.209. The bigger the run, the less aligned flow preceded it. For book absorption there is a real threshold and it is physical — and MNQ sits roughly 6× below it. For run size given a working entry, the threshold is about 195 points: NIPC went 8-for-8 green on the top-quartile runs and banked 68% of its run P&L from 38% of the runs.

3. On chop days, the OFF switch beats every gate I could build. The chop-day donation is −$249/day and it is recoverable with certainty by benching. The best chop-scalp I could construct out of 1,152 policies makes +$280/chop-day at t=1.50 — and the leave-one-day-out test says it will not repeat. Four vetoes it did validate are worth more to the router than the gate is.

★ HEADLINE — THE CHOP-DAY SCALP LAB: can we stop DONATING on chop days?

This was your explicit ask, so it goes first. Three days this week bled with the gates we have — Mon 07-27, Tue 07-28, Fri 07-31. The brief was blunt: build something that turns those days from a donation into +$200–300. I built it, swept it 1,152 ways, and I have to give you an honest NULL. Here is the whole argument, money first.

The bar, from the live ledger — not a vibe. Real desk P&L from gazbot7.db.trades:

DayRegimeLive tradesLive netRTH rangeRange efficiencyATR14
Mon 07-27CHOP (violent)37−$155678.500.57727.1
Tue 07-28CHOP (round-trip)25−$22476.500.07725.0
Wed 07-29trend32+$1,349782.250.67033.7
Thu 07-30trend82+$94484.750.70622.8
Fri 07-31CHOP (mixed)27−$569646.000.31727.3
3 chop daysthe donation89−$746= −$249 per chop day. That is the prize.
★ REV3 correction — 2026-08-01 — session-boundary note — this table and Part 1's day-by-day table are both right

This table books on the UTC calendar day; Part 1's day-by-day table books on the Paris session boundary the desk keeps its ledger on. Same trades, different day buckets — the week total is identical, the individual days are not. That is why Part 1 calls Thursday a +$1,172 record and this table prices the same Thursday at +$94. Add the hands-off cut and Thursday is the worst automated day of the week at −$884.5 on 73 fills, turned green by nine hand-claims.

One honest caveat before anything else: only 07-28 is a textbook round-trip chop day (range efficiency 0.077). 07-27 is a −392-point directional day that merely felt like chop because it got there in violent whipsaws, and 07-31 is mixed (0.317). That matters enormously — on two of the three target days there was no range to fade. It is the first reason "fade the turn" struggles.

The cost wall — why "scalp tiny, bank fast" is the wrong instinct. Total friction is 1.75 points per round-trip ($3.00 fee at the real $1.50/side, plus spread and slipped stops, MNQ $2/point). The fee is a fixed tax, so the smaller the target, the higher the hurdle:

ShapeNet winNet lossBreakeven win% required
Stop 20 pt, target 0.5R+$17−$43.5071.9%
Stop 20 pt, target 1.0R+$37−$43.5054.0%
Stop 30 pt, target 0.5R+$27−$63.5070.2%
Stop 30 pt, target 1.0R+$57−$63.5052.7%

The mechanical spec I tried. Candidate turn = every new 300-second local extreme, RTH+late (13:30–21:00 UTC), 30-second dedupe → 1,675 events over 6 days (279/day). Direction = fade (short the high, long the low). Entry marketable — a short hits the bid, a long lifts the ask. Target is a resting limit that needs a print through by a tick before it counts. Stop is stop-market, triggers on touch, fills a tick worse, and wins all ties inside a tick gap. Deliberately unkind, and it should be.

The per-trade edge — the single most important number in the lab. Stripping the fee out (keeping spread and slippage) to ask whether an edge exists at all:

StopTargetnWin%Gross pts/tradeNet $/trade
1.6×ATR0.75R38559.5%+1.81+$0.62
1.6×ATR0.50R49069.4%+1.63+$0.26
1.2×ATR1.00R45152.1%+0.26−$2.49
1.2×ATR0.50R62165.7%−0.51−$4.02
1.2×ATR1.50R37041.4%−2.25−$7.50

Best gross edge anywhere in the study = +1.81 points a trade. Round-trip friction = 1.75 points. The mean-reversion at a fresh local extreme is real — it is just 0.06 points net. The cost eats 97% of it. Cross-check by inversion: flip the identical signal to breakout-continuation and it makes −$5.29/trade against the fade's −$2.49 — a $2.80 spread ≈ 1.4 points of genuine reversion tendency, consistent. To clear +$250/chop-day at ~30 trades a day you need +$8.30/trade = 5.9 points gross. That is 3.3× the best edge measured anywhere in this lab.

The best thing 1,152 policies could produce — and why I still won't bank it. Grid: efficiency-ratio veto × ATR ceiling × range floor × stop multiple × R × hold, all sequential (one position at a time):

Grid statistic (1,152 sequential policies)Value
Mean chop-day P&L across all configs−$36
Standard deviation$131
Fraction with chop-day P&L > 040.9%
Fraction with $/trade > 018.8%
Best config, chop-day P&L+$288
Expected best-of-1,152 under a pure-noise null≈ +$455

Read the last two rows together: the best config out of 1,152 tries is below what random noise would be expected to produce from 1,152 tries. Marginal effects agree — averaged across the grid, no R and no stop multiple is meaningfully positive (best marginal: stop 1.2×ATR at +$37/chop-day; every R marginal is negative).

The best-of-grid config for the record — ATR ≤ 28, 30-min range ≥ 3.5×ATR, time ≥ 17:00 UTC, stop 1.2×ATR, R = 1.0, hold 600s:

Strip-the-best-trades on the chop days is genuinely healthy — strip 1 → +$260/day, strip 3 → +$225, strip 5 → +$192, strip 10 → +$113, and the single best chop trade is only +$61, so it is not one lucky print. But 07-28 alone supplies $454 of the $840, and a t of 1.50 selected from 1,152 trials is not evidence.

Two tests killed it outright.

Parallel → sequential collapseStacking the four validated vetoes on the raw event pool gives n=557, +$5.98/trade, +$1,145/chop-day, t=2.71. But those events overlap — with a 30s dedupe and a 900s hold you are notionally holding 30 positions and counting one move many times. Re-run honestly, one position at a time: n=101, +$3.57/trade, +$135/chop-day, t=0.66. A 3× collapse. Standing rule for the lab: every fader study must be scored sequentially.
Leave-one-day-out learnability76 features (price, stretch, flow, footprint, absorption, 10-deep book), 6 folds. For each held-out day, learn the single best feature+threshold on the other 5 days, apply it blind. In-sample mean +$7.17/trade → out-of-sample −$5.84/trade (n=318). An L2-regularised logistic on all 76 features, same protocol: OOS −$8.05/trade (n=530), correlations −0.011 / −0.093 / +0.059 / −0.087 / −0.004 / +0.027.
